I.

A New Way to Win

For two and a half thousand years, strategists have studied The Art of War. This book asks a different question.

The Old Way
兵家之道

Win by Conquest

Outmaneuver, outflank, outperform. Defeat the rival. Take the market. The world as a battlefield, competitors as enemies. Zero-sum: your gain is my loss.

Change 轉
The RuDao Way
儒商之道

Win by Co-opetition

Compete where it matters. Cooperate where it counts. Build partnerships that outlast the deal. Positive-sum: profit that endures because trust endures.

"Sun Tzu taught us how to defeat rivals. Confucius teaches us how to make rivals into partners."

— from the introduction

II.

About the Book

A business philosophy for leaders who refuse to choose between profit and principle.

In RuDao, Tony Tong weaves two and a half millennia of Confucian thought with the hard realities of modern commerce. The result is neither a self-help book nor a strategy manual — it is a way: a frame for decisions, partnerships, and growth that produces balanced profit and relationships that don't break when the cycle turns.

Drawing on his decades across finance, technology and cross-border enterprise, Tony shows how the Confucian virtues are not soft ideals but operational disciplines — the operating system of enduring business. The book is structured in six chapters — Ch.1 The Roots (源), Ch.2 The Confucian Core (儒), Ch.3 Ru + Dao (内圣外王), Ch.4 Inner Sage, Outer King and the RuJiang (儒将), Ch.5 RuShang Practice (实践), and Ch.6 RuDao in the AI Digital Age (数字时代) — from the Shang origins of commerce to the AI digital age, with Front Matter (Foreword, Purpose, How to Read) and Back Matter (Afterword, Appendices, References).

长久以来，《孙子兵法》被奉为商业博弈的经典，教会人们战胜对手、夺取胜利。但以击败竞争者为目标的零和思维，常常摧毁合作根基、损耗信任、难以维系长期伙伴关系。《儒道-儒将》跳出单纯对抗的商战逻辑，阐释源自儒家思想的竞合之道:不必在利润和情义之间二选一，在激烈竞争的同时，依然可以构建稳固、长久的伙伴网络。

For generations, The Art of War has been revered as the bible of business competition. Yet zero-sum thinking focused on beating opponents often undermines cooperation, erodes trust, and makes long-lasting partnerships nearly impossible. RuDao moves beyond pure adversarial strategy and unfolds the Confucian path of co-opetition — profit and meaningful relationships do not need to be trade-offs.

  • English SubtitleBeyond The Art of War

Preface

"Without Trust, a Man Cannot Stand"

人无信不立；君子和而不同 — Confucius 孔子

儒

"Without trust, a man cannot stand; the noble person seeks harmony, not conformity." — Confucius 孔子

As algorithms, blockchain, and artificial intelligence rewrite the underlying logic of global commerce, code can prove that data is authentic — but it cannot generate trust; smart contracts can execute rules — but they cannot guarantee the right rules, still less turn competitors into partners.

The first limit speaks to trust (xin 信). In an era where AI agents, smart contracts, and borderless digital nomads work together without ever meeting face to face, the trust Confucius insisted upon twenty-five centuries ago has become the scarcest foundational asset of the digital economy. The second speaks to harmony (he 和): the art of "the noble person seeks harmony, not conformity" is exactly the wisdom that turns competition into symbiosis and rivals into allies.

Only by standing on trust and living in harmony can digital commerce move from zero-sum games to enduring alliances. That is the core proposition of this book, RuDao — Beyond the Art of War: using an ancient merchant's way to answer a digital future.

In 1986, I graduated from King's College (Kings.edu.hk), Hong Kong — the same school that produced Professor D.C. Lau (Professor Din Cheuk Lau 劉殿爵教授), the authoritative English translator of The Analects 論語, Tao Te Ching 道德經 and Mencius 孟子 at the Chinese University of Hong Kong. As a junior alumnus of King's College, I was exposed to Confucian and Taoist culture from an early age through my teachers. From 2024–2026, I studied under Professor Xiao Xianhua (肖先华) and Mr. Zhang HaiBiao (张海标校长) in the HuaXue EnXue (HX357 Class 华学恩学) Chinese Studies, and received guidance from Taoist Master Ji Gong (季工 道长) of the Hong Kong Taoism Institute. I was inspired by Professor Tang Enjia (湯恩佳博士), President of the Hong Kong Confucian Academy (ConfucianAcademy.com 孔教學院院長).

My career has spanned Web1.0 to Web4: in 2000 I co-founded Pacificnet, a Nasdaq-listed company, and I hold a US Internet workflow patent. Today I teach AI, blockchain and Web3 at several universities, and have spoken at Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan, UBS and other global financial summits.

This dual experience — bridging traditional Confucianism, frontier technology and global finance — convinced me that Confucian thought is not a museum piece. It can provide a critical governance logic for the intelligent digital age.

The Art of War has long been treated as the bible of business strategy, teaching people how to defeat opponents. But zero-sum thinking, aimed at beating competitors, often destroys the foundations of cooperation, erodes trust, and makes long-term partnerships unsustainable.

This book steps beyond pure adversarial strategy and articulates the Confucian path of co-opetition (竞合) — the insight that one need not choose between profit and integrity. One can compete fiercely and still build a durable network of partners. Inner virtue alone is not enough to survive in a brutal global market; pure stratagem without ethical roots will eventually collapse. 儒商 (Ru-Shang) and 儒将 (Ru-Jiang) must work as one.

自序 · 人无信不立

「人无信不立；君子和而不同。」

"Without trust, a man cannot stand; the noble person seeks harmony, not conformity."
—— 孔子《论语》

当算法、区块链与人工智能重塑全球商业的底层逻辑，代码可以保证「数据为真」，却无法自动生成信任；智能合约可以执行规则，却无法保证规则正确，更无法让竞争对手成为伙伴。

前者关乎「信」——在这个 AI 智能体、智能合约与跨国数字游民从不线下谋面的时代，孔子两千五百年前所执着的「信」，反而成了数字经济最稀缺的底层资产。后者关乎「和」——「君子和而不同」的竞合之道，正是把竞争化为共生、把对手化为盟友的智慧。

唯有以「信」立身，以「和」共处，数字商业才能从零和博弈走向长久联盟。这正是写作本书《儒道 RuDao — Beyond the Art of War》的核心命题：用古老商道，回应数字未来。

当算法、区块链与人工智能重塑全球商业的底层逻辑，我们为什么仍要回望两千五百多年前的孔孟思想？这是写作本书时，我反复追问自己的核心命题。

我毕业于香港英皇书院（King's College），这所学校走出过《论语》权威英译者、香港中文大学教授刘殿爵 (DC Lau) 先生。作为刘教授的后辈校友，我自幼浸染儒家文化；其后又跟随华学体系创办人肖先華教授修习，并从香港孔教学院院长汤恩佳先生处深受启发。

我的职业生涯完整横跨了 Web1.0 到 Web3、Web4.0 的整个时代。2000年，我参与创办太平洋网（Pacificnet）并推动其于纳斯达克（Nasdaq）上市，拥有美国互联网工作流程发明专利；如今，我在多所高校主讲人工智能、区块链与 Web3 课程，并多次受邀在高盛、摩根大通、瑞银等国际金融机构峰会上发表主题演讲。

正是这种「东方儒学传统 × 前沿科技 × 全球金融实务」的跨界经历，让我日益确信:儒家思想并非束之高阁的古董，它恰恰能为智能数字时代提供一套关键的治理逻辑。

本书致力于搭建一座实操桥梁，打通《论语》的古典文本与二十一世纪的全球商业实践。它探讨仁、义、礼、智、信如何在 AI 伦理框架、DAO 去中心化组织与加密通证经济中获得当代诠释；也萃取古代、近代、当代儒商人物的真实案例，把「义利合一」的原则运用于跨境数字商业的竞争之中。

长久以来，《孙子兵法》被奉为商业博弈的经典，教导人们如何战胜对手。然而，以击败竞争者为核心的零和思维，往往摧毁合作的根基、损耗信任，令长期的伙伴关系难以为继。

因此，本书跳出单纯对抗的商战逻辑，阐释源于儒家思想的「竞合之道」——我们不必在利润与情义之间二选一，完全可以在激烈竞争的同时，构建稳固而长久的伙伴网络。仅有内在德行，不足以在残酷的全球市场立足；仅有谋略技巧，若缺失伦理根基，也终将走向崩塌。儒商与儒将，必须相辅相成、一体运行。

这不只是一部理论研究，更是一份行动指南。它面向企业家、投资人、技术从业者、学者，以及一切对「东方伦理如何面向数字未来」感兴趣的人。

唐仪 www.TonyTong.cc 2026 · Hong Kong

Co-Editors

RuDao 儒道 Co-Editors

编者 — Three co-editors who read the manuscript and wrote in its support.

编
01

Professor Kuang-Shih Yeh

RuDao Book Co-Editor · Ph.D. in Organization Theory, Carnegie Mellon University · Chair Professor, Chung Hua University

China has a long and profound commercial tradition. The Shang Dynasty, founded over three thousand years ago, was the earliest Chinese dynasty with written records. The word "Shang" itself stands for business, commerce, and merchants. It is said that the ancestors of the Shang people were highly adept at business and trade, which is how the dynasty came to be named "Shang".

Over two thousand years ago, the Records of the Grand Historian (Shiji史记) — the first book to systematically document Chinese history — included a dedicated chapter titled "Biographies of the Money-makers" (Huo Zhi Lie Chuan). Its core content focused on the path to wealth and the management wisdom of prominent historical merchants.

On the other hand, throughout successive dynasties, Confucianism and Taoism — the two most influential schools of thought shaping Chinese culture — have placed immense value on fundamental human principles and the philosophy of life. Chapter Two of this book, titled "The Eight Virtues," explores the essential ethical values familiar to every Chinese person. In traditional Chinese values, a respected and successful entrepreneur is honored as a "Confucian Entrepreneur" (Ru Shang). A Confucian Entrepreneur is not merely someone skilled at making money, but rather an individual who firmly upholds traditional Confucian core values while managing a business. I am delighted to see the author explain this concept of the "Confucian Entrepreneur" so clearly.

The author, Tony Tong, has received a complete education in both Western and Chinese traditions, possesses a deep understanding of cutting-edge technology, and brings a wealth of successful business experience. It can truly be said that he is a respected Confucian Entrepreneur himself.

By integrating the fundamental values and wisdom of Confucianism and Taoism into the modern business world, he emphasizes "co-opetition" rather than "zero-sum," proving that traditional Chinese wisdom remains fully applicable to today's complex commercial landscape. In an era marked by rapid technological advancements, complex dynamic environments, and geopolitical volatility, a guiding North Star is increasingly vital for business leaders. I am deeply grateful for Tony Tong's contribution in clearly articulating the wisdom of Confucian Entrepreneurs in this book. These insights serve as the North Star guiding us forward, making this a work well worth studying, whether or not you are actively engaged in the business world.

Professor Kuang-Shih Yeh　2026 / 10

About the Co-Editor

Professor Kuang-Shih Yeh holds a Bachelor's degree in Political Science from National Taiwan University, a Master's degree in Public Administration from the University of Delaware, and a Ph.D. in Organization Theory from Carnegie Mellon University. He currently serves as an Independent Director of Fubon Financial Holding Co., Ltd. and Clientron Corp, a Director of Pintrust Technology and Everjoy Health Group (Shanghai), Ltd., a Chair Professor at Chung Hua University, Honorary Chairman of the Taiwan Future Society, and Honorary Chairman of the Asia-Pacific Public-Private Partnership Association.

He has previously served as a Professor and Department Head of the Department of Business Management, Director of the Institute of Communication Management, Director of the Institute of Healthcare Management, and Executive Director of the EMBA program at National Sun Yat-sen University; Professor at the Institute of Technology Management and Intellectual Property Right at National Chengchi University; Visiting Professor at the School of Public Policy and Management, Tsinghua University (Beijing); and Visiting Professor at the School of Management, Fudan University (Shanghai). From 2008 to 2015 he also served in the public administration of the Taiwan region of China, including as Deputy Minister of the Research, Development and Evaluation Commission (RDEC), Political Deputy Minister and Minister of Transportation and Communications, first Chairman of Taoyuan International Airport Corporation, and later Vice Mayor of Kaohsiung City.

Additionally, he had served as an Independent Director for several companies, including Bank of Taiwan and Synnex Technology International Corp., Chief Operating Officer of Pacific Digital Media, and Vice Chairman of Shihlien Chemical Industrial Corp. He is the author of six management books, and has three times received the Golden Book Award from the Small and Medium Enterprise Administration, Ministry of Economic Affairs.

02

Thomas Cheung 張俊勇 MH JP

RuDao Book Co-Editor · HKBA Honorary Chairman · Former Delegate to the China National People's Congress (NPC) · Founding Partner, InnoAngel.com 香港英諾天使基金 · Council Member, Chinese General Chamber of Commerce Hong Kong · Co-Secretary-General, Hong Kong Blockchain Association (HKBA.cc)

I have known the author Tony Tong for over 10 years. Back between 1988 and 1991 we both attended universities in the United States, not far from one another. Tony studied Computer Engineering at the University of Minnesota (UMN.edu), while I pursued Applied Mathematics at the University of Wisconsin–Madison and later Cornell University (1991–1992).

In 2017, Tony and I co-founded the Hong Kong Blockchain Association (HKBA.cc), a leading industry body dedicated to blockchain application development, public education and regulatory advisory in Hong Kong. As an angel investor and partner at InnoAngel VC, I also invested in Com2000.org / 2000x.net, a prominent AI, blockchain and Bitcoin mining enterprise with operations across the United States.

This book RuDao goes far beyond The Art of War. It explores long-term co-opetition, enduring partnerships and cross-border strategic alliances. In today's AI, Web3 and blockchain era, we see an explosion of remote teams, distributed workers and digital nomads. Many of these cross-border collaborators, AI agents and digital avatars never meet face to face. Building trust and sustaining stable long-distance partnerships across different countries, cultures and legal systems has become one of the biggest challenges for global builders. This book delivers much-needed guidance for creating, maintaining and governing cross-border remote alliances.

At its core lies the principle of Yì Lì Hé Yī 义利合一 — Unity of Righteousness and Profit. This maxim is something I personally believe in and abide by in daily life, my investment decisions, and the management of all cross-border remote partnerships and alliances.

— Thomas Cheung MH JP　www.ThomasCheung.cc

03

Prof. Timmy Tan 陳建榮

RuDao Book Co-Editor · HKBA Philippines · Founder of LaIsla Magazine and SK Techno-lube Corporation · Honorary Professor, International University of Cooperative Education (iUCE) Niedersachsen, Germany · Alumni, Peking University Entrepreneurs Association · Managing Partner (Philippines), Global CyberSecurity Alliance (GCSA)

I am Timmy Tan, son of Lucio Tan, Chairman of the Federation of Filipino-Chinese Chambers of Commerce and Industry.

I have known the author Tony Tong for more than twenty years. I first met Tony when he was working with Pacificnet, a Web1 e-commerce company that Tony founded and took public on NASDAQ in 2000. This year, I joined HKBA and its strategic-alliance partner, Global CyberSecurity Alliance (GCSA.ai), as the Managing Partner for the Philippines.

My father Lucio Tan is a steadfast, lifelong practitioner of Ru-Dao and Confucian RuShang philosophy. He firmly lives by the principle of Righteousness before Profit (义利合一) and continuously draws wisdom from the Dao De Jing (道德经), advocating co-opetition and harmonious collaborative development. Across his decades building a global business empire spanning banking, aviation, tobacco, and beer, my father always believed that much of his lasting success came directly from Ru-Dao thinking.

During the 2015 APEC summit in Manila, Tony Tong met my father at my corporate gala dinner. My father shared that from childhood, he formed a lifelong habit of studying and memorizing traditional Chinese literary and philosophical classics, including the Analects (论语), The Art of War (孙子兵法), the Three-Character Classic (三字经), the 300 Tang Poems (唐诗300首), and the Romance of the Three Kingdoms (三国演义).

In 2002, my father was invited to deliver a keynote speech at the First International Confucian-Business Forum in Beijing. In his landmark address titled "Integrity is the Foundation for Establishing and Developing Enterprises," Lucio Tan stated that Cheng Xin (诚信), or trustworthiness and integrity, is the unshakable foundation of every enterprise. Even amid severe business crises, my father insisted that integrity must never be traded away for short-term survival.

Today's AI-Web3-blockchain world is filled with distributed remote teams, digital-nomad communities, cross-border partners, and AI digital-avatar collaborators who may never physically meet. Building durable trust for long-distance cross-border alliances has become one of the greatest challenges facing global entrepreneurs.

Tony Tong's new book, RuDao, brilliantly transforms the real, lived RuShang practice of my father into actionable modern-day guidance. It does not merely quote ancient texts. Instead, it connects age-old Chinese entrepreneurial wisdom to the real-world pain points of digital-era co-opetition and remote-alliance building. Every founder, investor, and cross-border alliance-builder should read this work.

Prof. Timmy Tan 陳建榮 · HKBA Philippines · RuDao Book Co-Editor

Son of Lucio Tan, the iconic Filipino-Chinese tycoon and Chairman of Philippine Airlines (PAL), Philippine National Bank (PNB), Allied Bank, and the Federation of Filipino-Chinese Chambers of Commerce and Industry. Source: China News Service, August 22, 2002

I dedicate this book to my parents: my father, Tong Cho Sam, engineer and Founder and Chairman of the Tong Family Foundation, and my mother, Cindy Yip, also an engineer and a graduate with a mechanical engineering degree from Guangxi University.

I am deeply thankful for the gift of my parents' nurturing and upbringing. My father graduated in 1965 from Shanghai Mechanical Institute, now renamed the University of Shanghai for Science and Technology (USST), as a qualified Electric-Mechanical Engineer. During his university years, he was an avid sportsman: a member of the basketball team, and a champion swimmer representing the university swimming team.

My father grew up and forged his path from HaiWuTian in Xing'an, Guilin, Guangxi. His life journey from HaiWuTian is documented in the biography "Songs Flying out of Haiwutian". He is an Honorary Citizen of Guilin, whose mountains and waters are praised as "most beautiful under heaven". Early in his career, he was a highly accomplished engineer working at Zhuzhou Instrument Factory in Hunan, making vital contributions to China's aerospace industry. As a distinguished EE/ME engineer, inventor and electronics entrepreneur, he took part in China's reform-and-opening-up entrepreneurial wave. He founded multiple electronics enterprises and factories across Hunan, Guangxi and Guangdong, specializing in television and related electronics industries, employing thousands of people and making substantial contributions to the society. He is also an accomplished painter skilled in drawing and Chinese calligraphy painting, as well as an accomplished Chinese dizi flute performer. He has performed dizi solos and collaborated with local orchestras on major stages at numerous middle schools and universities across Guilin.

I am forever grateful for my parents' decades of guidance by words and deeds. My father personally taught me the four arts of the Chinese scholar: Music 琴 qin, Chess 棋 qi, Calligraphy 书 shu, Painting 画 hua. He constantly encouraged me to endure hardship, holding firmly to the ancient maxim: "Only those who bear the hardest hardship may rise to greatness." He guided me toward balanced development of morality, intellect and physical fitness. Thanks to my parents' care, from childhood I received well-rounded training in academics, art, calligraphy, music, games of Chinese Chess and Go WeiQi, and sports including swimming, basketball, ice hockey and skiing. This balanced education nurtured my moral, intellectual and physical growth through the four arts and beyond.

I give thanks to my alma mater, King's College, Hong Kong. Its Chinese school motto is 慎思篤行, rendered in English as "Meticulous Thoughts, Diligent Action." This motto is more than a guiding phrase — it is a way of life. (School website: Kings.edu.hk)

I thank Master Teacher Xiao Xianhua 肖先华 of HuaXue-EnXue 华学恩学. Through his Huaxue lectures, I came to understand the path of self-cultivation through ritual, music and qin, which inspired the integrated Confucian-Daoist philosophy at the heart of this book.

I am also grateful to Principal Zhang Haibiao 张海标, who gave me profound insights into Confucian thought, RuShang Confucian-business ethics and traditional Chinese culture, especially the principles of "unity of righteousness and profit", and the long-lasting alliance cooperation business model.

Dedication: To Mayer Lutheran High School, University of Minnesota, USA

I dedicate this book, RuDao: Beyond The Art of War (RuDao.org), to my beloved United States alma mater, Mayer Lutheran High School (MayerLutheran.org). This dedication honors my senior year as an exchange student during the 1986–1987 academic year under the PEACE-USA Scholarship Program.

Special thanks go to my host families: the Harts and the Roger Lindemeier family. I also extend my deepest gratitude to my host brothers and classmates Eric and Matthew Lindemeier, Julie and Jessica Hart, and my classmates Chris and Erin Bode of Mayer Lutheran High School.

That 1986–1987 school year marked my very first time going abroad, flying halfway around the globe to the United States. I left behind my childhood home across Guilin, China, and Hong Kong. I still vividly remember my mother crying at the Hong Kong Airport departure hall, while my King's College classmates came to cheer and celebrate, as I was the first one in our class to study abroad.

Arriving in America, it was my first time experiencing a Western Christian culture. Living, studying, and playing alongside my American host families, I soon realized I was the only Chinese student in the school—and in the entire small town. The culture shock was profound as I traded Hong Kong's concrete forest of skyscrapers for the wide-open, corn-covered Great Plains of rural Midwest America.

Classwork quickly shaped my thinking. I took religious studies courses, attended daily chapel, and read classic Western literature. At the same time, athletics, fitness, and farm work built my character. As a member of the Mayer Lutheran Crusaders cross-country team, I trained intensely in the gym, wrestling, weight-lifting, basketball camp, and hockey, eventually earning awards in cross-country tournaments. Running across endless corn and alfalfa fields, I learned to bale hay, harvest alfalfa, and work on local dairy farms. This hands-on farm life strengthened my resilience.

We lived in Mayer and New Germany, Minnesota, a region populated mostly by hard-working, Protestant residents of German descent. My host families immersed me deeply in the rhythms of Midwestern farm life, assigning me plenty of chores like baling hay and mowing the lawns for our local church, Zion Lutheran, and its cemetery. Baling heavy square hay bales—averaging 40 to 75 pounds (18 to 34 kg)—in the brutal Minnesota summer was grueling, legendary work. Managing the church and cemetery grounds added another 10 acres of exhausting lawn to mow. Yet, pairing this tiring physical labor with caring for a community cornerstone captured the heart of the region's heritage and taught me the Christian way of hard work before pleasure. I still fondly remember resting after those long days, listening to Garrison Keillor's Minnesota Public Radio show, A Prairie Home Companion. I giggled at its famous sign-off about the fictional town of Lake Wobegon: "where the women are strong, the men are good-looking, and all the children are above average."

Minnesota winters brought entirely new adventures with my host families and classmates. I play-wrestled with the Lindemeier brothers in the backyard snowdrifts and played hockey on our homemade backyard rink, which we built by flooding the lawn and stacking left-over hay bales for walls. We also ventured out across the state for classic winter traditions, cross-country skiing, ice-fishing, and playing pond hockey on the frozen surfaces of local spots like Lake Waconia, the city's scenic Lake of the Isles, and even taking trips up to the frozen shores of Lake Superior.

Through this lifestyle, I experienced the Lutheran philosophy of "hard work before pleasure"—a practical living out of Martin Luther's concept of "The Calling" (Beruf). In this community, secular farm work was a religious duty and a way to serve God. It perfectly mirrored the spirit of worldly asceticism, where economic diligence and self-discipline are signs of faith, and personal pleasure is set aside for a higher purpose. This faith-driven resilience is precisely what communities supporting Mayer Lutheran High School continue to build today.

My time at Mayer Lutheran laid the foundational groundwork for my later thesis study of Max Weber's The Protestant Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism. In my RuDao book, I merge this Western Protestant view with the Eastern Ru-Shang (Confucian-Merchant) principle of righteousness and profit coexisting (义利合一), demonstrating how faith, ethics, and economic diligence unite across different cultures.

To this day, I hold warm memories of daily chapel, Sunday church services, and Bible study in English, Hebrew, and Latin. I recall reading great works like Beowulf, Shakespeare, Hemingway, S. E. Hinton's The Outsiders, and Tolkien's The Lord of the Rings and The Hobbit. One of my brightest youthful memories remains touring with our school orchestra all the way from Minnesota down to Houston, Texas.

I am deeply grateful for Mayer Lutheran's academic guidance and spiritual mentorship. These gifts have stayed with me across decades, and I am proud to pay tribute to my alma mater, my host families, and my MLHS friends and classmates in this book.

1987–1991: University of Minnesota and the Dawn of Web 0.9

Following my time at Mayer Lutheran, I attended the University of Minnesota - Twin Cities, where I pursued a Mechanical-Industrial Engineering major paired with dual minors in Computer Science and Business Management, graduating in 1991.

It was during this monumental transition at the turn of the decade that the architecture of the modern digital landscape first began to take shape. Following Tim Berners-Lee's initial 1989 CERN proposal, I was fortunate to be among the absolute earliest technophiles to dive deep into the raw HTTP v0.9 protocol documentation. Poring over those original technical manuals gave me a deep understanding of stateless hypertext transfers over TCP/IP. This early architectural clarity proved invaluable, allowing me to quickly master the emergence of the Common Gateway Interface (CGI) specification when it debuted in 1993, and providing the precise structural mechanics needed to engineer pioneering web-to-database connections directly with SQL backends.

My journey in Minnesota also paved the way for my family. My brother, Victor Tong (唐滔), enrolled at the University of Minnesota in 1989, joining me on campus. Embracing our family's shared spirit of dedication, he excelled deeply in his academic pursuits, officially graduating with Cum Laude honors in 1993 with a Bachelor of Science in Physics. His hard work and leadership culminated in winning the prestigious Student Commencement Speaker Award. Standing before his fellow graduates as the UMN Class of 1993 graduation speaker, he delivered a moving commencement address inspired by the legacy of Martin Luther King Jr.—a proud milestone that beautifully bridged my parents' engineering heritage with the Protestant values of hard work and social responsibility.

Following his graduation, Victor and I both entered the corporate ranks of Andersen Consulting, a premier global IT consulting heavyweight that later rebranded as Accenture. This was a highly prestigious launchpad for our careers, allowing us to combine our technical IT background with high-stakes enterprise systems for America's top Fortune 500 corporations. Operating under the direct supervision of Managing Director Michael Gaard, we spearheaded complex IT system integration and corporate consulting projects for powerhouse Midwestern clients, including U.S. Bank (USBank.com), Northwest Airlines, Best Buy, and Target (Target.com). This intensive corporate exposure served as our practical introduction to the engine of modern American commerce, providing the precise operational discipline that would later fuel our own international ventures.

1993–2000: From Minnesota Ice to the Nasdaq DotCom Boom

The years following the 1993 birth of CGI up through 2000 opened up an exciting new chapter of internet startup entrepreneurship, building dot-com web applications, and serving local client connections. Between 1993 and 1995, my brother Victor and I worked closely with Andy Mullin, Charles Wilson's CultureShock, and the visionary Minneapolis developer brothers JJ and Jeremy Allaire. Together, we were deeply immersed in early web applications middleware development—pioneering the integration of front-end web browser applications with back-end CGI database servers and navigating the absolute technical frontiers of web-to-database connectivity.

During this dawn of commercial web development, Minneapolis became a prominent regional hub for CF middleware innovation. The Allaire brothers famously engineered early database-to-web connectors right here in the Twin Cities, founding Allaire Corporation in Minnesota in 1995. They pioneered the first database-driven web-content servers under the product name Cold Fusion (initially spelled with a space). Using these newly emerging web-to-database development environments with CGI and SQL connections, I witnessed firsthand as they set the stage for monumental success. With the breakthrough release of version 4.0 in 1998, the space was officially dropped, changing the product name permanently to ColdFusion. By this time, the platform was rapidly expanding into a global powerhouse, capturing a booming generation of hundreds of thousands of web developers worldwide.

This momentum culminated in January 1999 when Allaire Corporation launched a highly successful Initial Public Offering (IPO) on the Nasdaq under the ticker ALLR. The platform's immense value led to the Macromedia Era in March 2001, when Macromedia absorbed and merged with Allaire Corporation in a transaction valued at approximately $360 million. Following the acquisition, the software became known as Macromedia ColdFusion, and co-founder Jeremy Allaire stepped into the corporate leadership role of Chief Technology Officer (CTO) for Macromedia. The software's lineage reached its definitive modern state during the Adobe Era in December 2005, when tech giant Adobe Systems acquired Macromedia for $3.4 billion in stock, transitioning the product family permanently to Adobe ColdFusion.

Jeremy Allaire went on to co-found Circle (Circle.com) in 2013, launching the premier digital dollar stablecoin USDC in 2018. This second tech empire reached its ultimate milestone in June 2025, when Circle Internet Group executed a massive, historic Initial Public Offering under the ticker CRCL. After pricing at $31 per share and raising $1.05 billion, the stock exploded by nearly 250% on its debut, rocketing its valuation to $18 billion. By late 2026, driven by institutional adoption and its expanded agentic AI tech stack, Circle's market capitalization surged past $22 billion. I personally reached out to congratulate Jeremy Allaire on this phenomenal achievement—a staggering, full-circle evolution from our humble beginnings in 1993, when we all shared a tiny, free office space in Minneapolis, purely driven by raw tech passion and the spirit of innovation.

Inspired by this mid-90s boom, Victor Tong carved out his own path by developing a highly competitive, cross-operating-system alternative database middleware server named WebPlus.com (Web+), it offered a fast, robust alternative engine for commercial web apps, and WebPlusShop, which remains a popular e-commerce storefront platform across the United States today.

1999–2000: The Pacificnet Ecommerce Nasdaq Listing

The years between 1999 and 2000 marked a historic transition for our entrepreneurial journey, as Pacificnet evolved from a Minneapolis startup into a premier cross-border e-commerce CRM portal bridging the United States and Hong Kong.

I owe an immense debt of gratitude to my ex-cousin-in-law, Andrew "Andy" Mullin, and the wonderful Mullin family. Andy, who began serving as the Mayor of Wayzata, Minnesota in 2025, played a pivotal role in my early career. He welcomed me into his competitive young business hockey team, introducing me to the unique camaraderie of Minnesota's corporate ice leagues, and graciously brought me into his extensive family gatherings within prominent Minneapolis business circles. I am incredibly proud of Andy for becoming such a trusted business and political leader for the City of Wayzata.

It was through Andy's robust network that I met his close friend Ted Farrell at Haskells, a venerable Twin Cities institution. They became one of our very first internet web design clients when we helped design the Haskells.com e-commerce website—the first-of-its-kind wine e-commerce platform in town! As our relationship grew into deep mutual trust, the leadership at Haskell's transitioned from clients to believers, becoming one of the earliest angel investors in my first internet enterprise, Pacificnet.

Pacificnet started its roots in Minneapolis before strategically moving its core operations to Hong Kong to capture the booming international trade highway. That early foundation of trust, local networking, and collaborative capital eventually propelled Pacificnet all the way to a successful public listing on the Nasdaq stock exchange in June 2000.

I am forever thankful to Andy Mullin, the Mullin family, and the Farrell family for their invaluable contributions of time, effort, and early funding in our founding days. It remains a powerful, real-world lesson in how strong personal ethics, local networks, and mutual respect build truly sustainable global enterprises.

献言 Dedication

谨以此书献给父亲唐楚三（Tong Cho Sam），唐楚三家族基金会创始人主席及工程师，与母亲叶金雀（Cindy Yip），工程师，毕业于广西大学。

感念父母养育深恩。父亲 1963 年毕业于上海机械学院（Shanghai Mechanical College，今更名上海理工大学 University of Shanghai for Science and Technology, USST），为机电工程师（EEME, Electric-Mechanical Engineer）。在校期间，父亲热爱体育运动，是校篮球队队员，亦是校游泳队代表，斩获游泳赛事冠军。

父亲出身广西桂林兴安海武田，从这片乡土奋斗起家；记述他人生事迹的传记著作《从海武田飞出的歌》，记录了他从海武田走出的奋斗历程。父亲是桂林荣誉市民，早年作为专业且颇有建树的工程师，任职湖南株洲仪表厂，为中国航天事业作出重要贡献；作为 EE/ME 工程师、发明家与电子行业企业家，投身国家改革开放创业浪潮，于湖南、广西、广东创办多家电子企业与工厂，深耕电视机等电子产业，曾聘用数千名员工，为国家与社会作出重要贡献。他亦是造诣不俗的笛子演奏者、书画家与书法家，多次登上桂林多所中学、大学大型舞台，与中国民族乐团同台合奏笛乐。

感恩父母数十年言传身教，父亲亲手教我琴棋书画。他一直勉励我吃苦耐劳，笃信"吃得苦中苦，方为人上人"，并引导我追求德、智、体全面发展。得益于父母的栽培，我自幼便在体育——游泳、篮球、冰球、滑雪，以及音乐、绘画、书法、象棋、围棋，即琴棋书画诸艺之中，获得德、智、体均衡完整的教育与成长。

感谢我的母校香港英皇书院 Kings.edu.hk，校训为"慎思篤行"，英文译为 "Meticulous Thoughts, Diligent Action"。这句校训不只是一句指引格言，更是一种生活践行之道。

感谢华学-恩学的肖先华老师——汉朝开国相国萧何七十四世孙。承蒙肖老师讲授华学，令我体悟礼乐与琴道的修身法门，启发本书儒道一体的核心思考。

同时感恩张海标校长，在儒家思想、儒商精神与中华传统文化层面，给予我关于"义利合一、竞合模式"的深刻启发。

Tony Tong
