Chapter 1

The Roots 源 · Natural Pursuit of Profit 人性趋利 from 1600 BCE

1.1 — Shang (c. 1600–1046 BCE): Business Begins 商朝 · 商业起源

源

「天下熙熙，皆为利来；天下攘攘，皆为利往。」

"All under heaven bustles and hustles, coming and going — all for profit."
— Sima Qian, Records of the Grand Historian · Shiji史记・货殖列传 — Biographies of the Money-makers

More than three millennia ago, before the Great Wall rose or Confucius was born, the first Chinese merchants had already taken to the roads and rivers of the Yellow River valley, carrying goods, coin, and ideas that would quietly help shape a civilization. Commerce was never merely a footnote to Chinese history — it helped write it.

China possesses one of the world's oldest and most enduring commercial traditions. Its origins reach back more than three millennia to the Shang 商 Dynasty (c. 1600–1046 BCE) — the earliest Chinese dynasty to have left behind written records. Significantly, the very character Shāng 商 — which in modern Chinese denotes business, commerce, and merchants — is inseparable from this dynasty's legacy. According to a long-held tradition, the ancestors of the Shang people were celebrated for their skill in trade and commerce, a reputation so pronounced that the dynasty bore their name, and the term ShāngRén 商人 ("merchant") passed into the language to describe those who conduct trade.

Over two thousand years later, Sima Qian's Records of the Grand Historian (Shiji史记) — the first work to systematically chronicle Chinese history — devoted a dedicated chapter, "Biographies of the Money-makers" (Huòzhì Lièzhuàn 史记・货殖列传), to the pursuit of wealth and the managerial wisdom of history's most accomplished merchants.

Alongside this commercial heritage stand the two philosophical traditions that most profoundly shaped Chinese culture — Confucianism 儒 and Daoism 道 — which have consistently exalted fundamental human principles and a philosophy of principled living. Chapter Two of this book, "The Eight Virtues" (BāDé 八德), examines the essential ethical values familiar to every Chinese person. Within traditional Chinese values, an entrepreneur who earns both respect and success while conducting business with integrity is honored as a "Confucian Entrepreneur" (RúShāng 儒商). Such a person is not merely one who excels at generating profit, but an individual who firmly upholds the core values of the Confucian tradition in every aspect of enterprise.

Acknowledgment 致谢

My deepest gratitude goes to my dear friend and wise teacher, Professor Kuang-Shih Yeh 葉匡時教授, for his invaluable contribution to the opening chapter of RuDao 儒道. Professor Yeh holds a Bachelor's degree in Political Science from National Taiwan University, a Master's degree in Public Administration from the University of Delaware, and a Doctorate in Organization Theory from Carnegie Mellon University.

I am thankful that my teacher Prof. Kuang-Shih Yeh has articulated his concept of the Confucian Entrepreneur and the origin of China's Shang-business with such clarity.

Chapter 2.2

The Eight Virtues of the RuShang 儒商八德

A System of Quotations — Ren 仁 · Yi 义 · Li 礼 · Zhi 智 · Xin 信 · Zhong 忠 · Xiao 孝 · Shu 恕

德

"The gentleman takes righteousness as his substance, practices it through propriety, expresses it with humility, and perfects it with trustworthiness. Such is the gentleman!"

君子义以为质，礼以行之，孙以出之，信以成之。君子哉！

— The Analects · Wei Ling Gong 论语·卫灵公

"The feeling of compassion is the beginning of benevolence; the feeling of shame and dislike is the beginning of righteousness; the feeling of modesty and yielding is the beginning of propriety; the feeling of right and wrong is the beginning of wisdom."

恻隐之心，仁之端也；羞恶之心，义之端也；辞让之心，礼之端也；是非之心，智之端也。

— Mencius · GongSun Chou I 孟子·公孙丑上

The Eight Virtues are the complete operating system of the RuShang personality. They are not abstract moral items but a "moral algorithm" that maps, virtue by virtue, onto concrete business decisions.

The Eight Virtues at a Glance

VirtuePinyinEnglishBusiness Meaning
仁RénBenevolencePeople-centered; altruistic symbiosis
义YìRighteousnessRighteousness first; run the business the right way
礼LǐProprietyRules and compliance; orderly operation
智ZhìWisdomDiscern right from wrong; think and innovate
信XìnTrustworthinessA promise worth a thousand gold; reputation as capital
忠ZhōngLoyaltyDuty and dedication; accountability to nation and society
孝XiàoFilial PietyHonor teachers and roots; carry the culture forward
恕ShùReciprocityEmpathy and inclusiveness; win together

The general principle: the RuShang takes Ren as substance, Yi as essence, Li as measure, Zhi as instrument, Xin as foundation, Zhong as duty, Xiao as root, and Shu as the Way. Only with all eight is one a true RuShang.

Ren 仁: The Altruistic Substrate of Business

Mencius said: "The feeling of compassion is the beginning of benevolence." Ren is not weakness — it is the business ethic of "treating people as people."

"A benevolent person helps others establish what they wish to establish, and helps others achieve what they wish to achieve."

夫仁者，己欲立而立人，己欲达而达人。

— The Analects · Yong Ye 论语·雍也

In business: the enterprise must not only succeed itself — it must help employees, customers, and suppliers succeed. The benevolent has no enemies, because the benevolent builds irreplaceable partner trust.

Yi 义: The Moral Boundary of Profit

"A gentleman understands righteousness; a petty person understands only profit."

君子喻于义，小人喻于利。

— The Analects · Li Ren 论语·里仁

Yi is the criterion of "should this be done at all?" The RuShang does not refuse profit — profit must simply come by paths that righteousness permits.

Li 礼: Compliance and Order

"Without learning propriety, one cannot take a stand."

不学礼，无以立。

— The Analects · Ji Shi 论语·季氏

In modern commerce, Li means institutions, contracts, compliance, and industry norms. To operate by Li is not ritual formalism — it is building fair, transparent rules of cooperation.

Zhi 智: The Insight to Tell Right from Wrong

"The wise are free from perplexity, the benevolent from worry, the courageous from fear."

知者不惑，仁者不忧，勇者不惧。

— The Analects · Zi Han 论语·子罕

Zhi is more than data analysis and market judgment — it is the capacity to tell right from wrong, good from evil. ZiGong's "predictions often proved correct" combined commercial intelligence with moral intelligence.

Xin 信: The Foundation That Crosses Cycles

"A person without trustworthiness — I do not know what they are good for."

人而无信，不知其可也。

— The Analects · Wei Zheng 论语·为政

Xin is the RuShang's hardest soft power. Keeping trust in good times is easy; keeping it in adversity is the true touchstone.

Zhong 忠: Duty and Accountability

"Do not be weary in office; carry out duties with loyalty."

居之无倦，行之以忠。

— The Analects · Yan Yuan 论语·颜渊

Zhong is not blind obedience — it is dedication to the cause, to partners, and to country. For the modern RuShang, Zhong forms a closed loop of responsibility to shareholders, employees, customers, and society.

Xiao 孝: Heritage and Roots

"Filial piety and fraternal respect are the root of benevolence!"

孝悌也者，其为仁之本与！

— The Analects · Xue Er 论语·学而

Within the enterprise, Xiao becomes respect for mentors, carrying forward the culture, honoring those who came before. A person without respect for roots can hardly build genuine cultural cohesion.

Shu 恕: The Golden Rule of Empathy

"Do not impose on others what you do not wish for yourself."

己所不欲，勿施于人。

— The Analects · Wei Ling Gong 论语·卫灵公

Shu is the meta-rule of all RuShang business relationships. Negotiation, contracts, supply chains, competition, crisis — concrete conduct in all of them can be derived from this one sentence.

Quote Wall 名言墙

Mencius: "Treat the elders of your own family with reverence, and extend it to the elders of all families; treat the young of your own family with kindness, and extend it to the young of all families." 老吾老以及人之老，幼吾幼以及人之幼。

Confucius: "Be strict with yourself and lenient in blaming others, and resentment will stay far away." 躬自厚而薄责于人，则远怨矣。

The Great Learning: "The gentleman has his measuring square and compass." 君子有絜矩之道也。

Inamori Kazuo: "Revere heaven and love people." 敬天爱人。

Entrepreneur's Action Checklist 行动清单

  • Turn the Eight Virtues into a set of corporate values cards; focus on one virtue per quarter.
  • Before every major decision, score it against the Eight Virtues self-check sheet.
  • Establish an internal "Reciprocity Day" 恕道日 to encourage cross-departmental empathy.
  • In the employee handbook, concretize the righteousness–profit boundary as ten prohibited behaviors.

The Eight Virtues were not designed in a vacuum — behind them lies Confucianism's 2,500-year self-renewal. Only by reading this river of evolution can we understand why the virtues still work today.

Chapter 2.4

The Five Constants in Market Competition 仁义礼智信

Ren 仁 · Yi 义 · Li 礼 · Zhi 智 · Xin 信 — the WuChang 五常 as the Ethical Code of Enterprise

常

"The benevolent are free from worry, the wise from perplexity, the courageous from fear."

仁者不忧，知者不惑，勇者不惧。

— The Analects · Zi Han

"Know the enemy and know yourself, and you will not be imperiled in a hundred battles."

知彼知己，百战不殆。

— The Art of War · Mou Gong

"One who has the Way will have many supporters; one who has lost the Way will have few supporters."

得道者多助，失道者寡助。

— Mencius · GongSun Chou II

The RuJiang is not a literatus who moralizes in the abstract, but a strategist who carries the Confucian Five Constants (WuChang 五常) into real market competition. The Five Constants (WuChang 五常) was formulated by Dong Zhongshu (董仲舒 179–104 BCE) in the Western Han Dynasty.

The Five Virtues did not arrive all at once. Confucius planted Ren as the master thread, speaking often of Yi, Li, and Xin, but had not yet fixed the five as a canon. Mencius named the four beginnings:

"Compassion is benevolence; shame is righteousness; respect is propriety; discernment of right and wrong is wisdom. These four virtues are innate in human nature, not imposed from outside."

恻隐之心，仁也；羞恶之心，义也；恭敬之心，礼也；是非之心，智也。仁义礼智，非由外铄我也，我固有之也。

— Mencius · Gao Zi I

In the Western Han, Dong Zhongshu 董仲舒 added Xin and named them together as the Way of the Five Constants:

"Benevolence, righteousness, propriety, wisdom, and trustworthiness are the Way of the Five Constants, which rulers must cultivate and uphold."

夫仁谊（义）礼知（智）信，五常之道，王者所当修饬也。

— Book of Han · Biography of Dong Zhongshu

The White Tiger Hall Conference (79 CE) of the Eastern Han fixed the canon, and the Three Character Classic carried it through a millennium of schooling: "Ren and Yi, Li, Zhi and Xin — these five constants admit no disorder." Confucius planted the seed; Mencius assembled four; Dong Zhongshu added Trustworthiness to complete the Five Constants — the ethical backbone this book carries into market competition.

Ren 仁: The Irreplaceable Partner Trust

"The benevolent person loves others."

仁者爱人。

— Mencius · Li Lou II

Benevolence is not weakness — it is the deepest moat. Treat employees, customers, and partners well, and you earn a loyalty that money cannot buy.

Case: In the JAL rebuild, Inamori Kazuo cut no jobs and no salaries; "revere heaven, love people" roused the whole workforce, and the turnaround came in a V-shape within two-plus years. This is the modern business proof of "the benevolent has no enemies."

Yi 义: The Dynamic Balance of Profit and Righteousness

"When seeing profit, think of righteousness."

见利思义。

— The Analects · Xian Wen

The RuJiang pursues profit but refuses to trade righteousness for short-term gain. Before every major decision, ask: does this accord with Yi? (The five righteousness-profit boundaries are given in full in Section 5.4 — harm to user health, exploitation of the vulnerable, environmental destruction, fraud through information asymmetry, monopoly corrosion of the industry. Check each before deciding.)

Li 礼: Fair and Transparent Rules of Cooperation

"Without learning propriety, one cannot take a stand."

不学礼，无以立。

— The Analects · Ji Shi

Li stands for rules, institutions, processes. A clear, fair, predictable cooperation framework lowers transaction friction and increases partners' sense of security. The RuJiang's three principles of Li: state the rules of cooperation in advance — no turning the table afterwards; allocate benefits transparently — no back-room deals; provide channels for resolving conflict — no surprise attacks.

Zhi 智: Reading the Market's Data — and Human Nature

"The wise are free from perplexity."

知者不惑。

— The Analects · Zi Han

Read the data rationally, and also read the trust, fear, and hope inside human nature. ZiGong's "predictions often proved correct" joined commercial intelligence to moral intelligence.

Type of WisdomContentApplication
Data intelligenceMarket analysis, financial models, trend judgmentRational decisions
Human intelligenceUnderstanding motives, fears, trustNegotiation, alliances
Moral intelligenceTelling right from wrong; conscienceRighteousness-profit boundaries

Xin 信: Crossing Cycles on Credit

"A person without trustworthiness — I do not know what they are good for."

人而无信，不知其可也。

— The Analects · Wei Zheng

Keeping trust in prosperity is easy; keeping it in adversity is the RuJiang's true touchstone. Three layers of credit value: lower transaction costs (partners need no costly due diligence); priority access to opportunity (good deals go to the trusted first); rescue in crisis (the reputable find helping hands).

The Five Constants United: The RuJiang Strategic Framework

VirtueCore CapabilityMarket Application
Ren 仁Building trustPartner loyalty; employee cohesion
Yi 义Drawing boundariesCompliance; never taking ill-gotten gains
Li 礼Building rulesContracts, institutions, processes
Zhi 智Insight and judgmentMarket analysis; reading human nature
Xin 信Long-term creditBrand, goodwill, crisis resilience

Confucius: "The benevolent person loves others." 仁者爱人。

Confucius: "When seeing profit, think of righteousness." 见利思义。

Mencius: "One who has the Way will have many supporters." 得道者多助，失道者寡助。

SunTzu: "Know the enemy and know yourself." 知彼知己，百战不殆。

  • Assess your company's competitive capability with the Five Constants framework; find the weakest link.
  • Build a "righteousness-profit balance" decision list; every major decision must pass the Yi-Li review.
  • Optimize cooperation processes so rules are transparent and benefit-sharing fair.
  • Maintain a "credit capital" ledger recording the company's integrity assets and losses.
  • The Five Constants are established in the heart but must be completed in technique — and the most frequent examination hall is the negotiating table and the forging of alliances.
  • The Inner Sage and Outer King now converge: RuShang and RuJiang were always two faces of one person.
Chapter 4

Inner Sage, Outer King, and the RuJiang 内圣外王 · 儒将

The Philosophical Unity of RuShang and RuJiang — NeiShengWaiWang

王

A Complete Business System for the Digital Age

RuShang and RuJiang are not sequential steps but two faces of one whole. This part extends the framework to AI, Web3, DAO, and the token economy, and weaves in the civilizational dialogue of Toynbee and Ikeda together with the scholar-merchant theory of HuaXue-EnXue — a complete solution for globalized digital business.

Inner Sage, Outer King: The Philosophical Unity of RuShang and RuJiang

"The ancients who wished to illustrate illustrious virtue throughout the world first governed their states; wishing to govern their states, they first regulated their families; wishing to regulate their families, they first cultivated themselves."

古之欲明明德于天下者，先治其国；欲治其国者，先齐其家；欲齐其家者，先修其身。

— The Great Learning (DaXue 大学)

"Cultivate oneself to bring peace to others."

修己以安人。

— The Analects · Xian Wen

"When poor, cultivate oneself alone; when successful, benefit the whole world."

穷则独善其身，达则兼济天下。

— Mencius · Jin Xin I

RuShang and RuJiang are not two professions — they are two faces of the same person. The Inner Sage is the root of the Outer King; the Outer King is the practice of the Inner Sage. Without the Inner Sage, the Outer King degenerates into scheming. Without the Outer King, the Inner Sage remains empty talk.

Reading "Inner Sage, Outer King" 内圣外王

The term first appears in the Zhuangzi · Tian Xia chapter and was later absorbed by Confucianism as the complete expression of the ideal personality.

Inner Sage = RuShang: self-cultivation, the righteousness-profit discernment, the ground tone of character.

Outer King = RuJiang: market competition, alliance-building, co-opetition strategy.

The two cannot be severed.

DimensionInner Sage (RuShang)Outer King (RuJiang)
FocusInner characterOuter achievement
Core questionWho am I? What do I hold to?How do I act? What do I accomplish?
KeywordsRen, Yi, Li, Zhi, XinCo-opetition, negotiation, alliance, crisis
ExpressionCultivation, integrity, benevolenceStrategy, execution, leadership

The Complete Growth Path

The Great Learning gives the ladder: investigate things (GeWu 格物) → extend knowledge (ZhiZhi 致知) → make the intention sincere (ChengYi 诚意) → rectify the heart (ZhengXin 正心) → cultivate the self (XiuShen 修身) → regulate the family (QiJia 齐家) → govern the state (ZhiGuo 治国) → bring peace to the world (PingTianXia 平天下).

Mapped onto modern business: personal cultivation (sincere intention, rectified heart) → team governance → running the enterprise → market co-opetition (the RuJiang's Outer King) → giving back to industry and society. Each step rests on the previous; none can be skipped. Strategy without self-cultivation is a rootless tree; social responsibility without strategy is a castle in the air.

The Four Unities

The core of the RuShang–RuJiang system is the realization of four unities:

Unity one: righteousness and profit. "Righteousness generates profit; profit benefits the people." — Righteousness and profit can be held together, and only together do they last.

Unity two: competition and cooperation. "Gentlemen harmonize while maintaining differences." (Analects · Zi Lu) — Cooperation inside competition, competition inside cooperation. Co-opetition is not expedient; it is long-term strategy.

Unity three: the short term and the long term. "If a person has no long-term concerns, he will surely have near-term worries." (人无远虑，必有近忧。 Analects · Wei Ling Gong) — Short-term tactics serve long-term strategy; never sacrifice tomorrow's great righteousness for today's small gain.

Unity four: personal success and social contribution. "When successful, benefit the whole world." — Enterprise success and social progress advance together. The true RuJiang does not merely make money for himself; he creates value for industry, society, and country.

The Modern Business Reading of NeiShengWaiWang

Classical StepModern Business Correspondence
Investigate things, extend knowledge (格物致知)Market research, technology study, data insight
Sincere intention, rectified heart (诚意正心)Values building; corporate culture
Cultivate the self (修身)Leadership discipline; integrity building
Regulate the family (齐家)Team management; organization building
Govern the state (治国)Enterprise management; industry leadership
Peace to the world (平天下)Global business civilization; social responsibility

Why the Digital Age Needs "Inner Sage, Outer King" All the More

Digital technology magnifies human capability — and magnifies human error. An AI enterprise without the Inner Sage may use algorithms to split society; a RuShang without the Outer King may be swept away by the technological tide. The digital age demands: technological innovation with a moral foundation, and ethical steadfastness with strategic capability. That is the contemporary meaning of NeiShengWaiWang.

The Great Learning: "The ancients who wished to illustrate illustrious virtue first cultivated themselves." 古之欲明明德于天下者，先治其国。

Confucius: "Cultivate oneself to bring peace to others." 修己以安人。

Mencius: "When poor, cultivate oneself alone; when successful, benefit the whole world." 穷则独善其身，达则兼济天下。

Confucius: "No long-term concerns, sure near-term worries." 人无远虑，必有近忧。

  • Draw the "Inner Sage, Outer King" growth-path map for yourself and your company.
  • Check the four unities — righteousness/profit, competition/cooperation, short/long, individual/society — and find the weakest.
  • Convert "cultivate–regulate–govern–pacify" into the hierarchy of your company's mission and vision.
  • In digital transformation, embed ethics review into the technology development process.
  • NeiShengWaiWang is not a classical relic — in the AI era it has found its sharpest examination hall: algorithm governance.
Chapter 4.3

The Art of War (Sun Tzu) vs Co-opetition 竞合之道

The Boundary of SunTzu and the Way of Co-opetition — Turning Counterparties into Partners

合

"Warfare is the way of deception."

兵者，诡道也。

— The Art of War · Shi Ji (Laying Plans)

"To win a hundred victories in a hundred battles is not the highest excellence; to subdue the enemy without fighting is the highest excellence."

百战百胜，非善之善者也；不战而屈人之兵，善之善者也。

— The Art of War · Mou Gong (Attack by Stratagem)

"The gentleman has no contentions. If there must be contention, let it be in archery. They bow and yield to each other before ascending, and drink together after descending. Even in contention, they remain gentlemen."

君子无所争，必也射乎！揖让而升，下而饮，其争也君子。

— The Analects · Ba Yi

The Art of War is a great military classic — that is fact. But reading it directly as a business bible is a dangerous misreading. This chapter does not repudiate SunTzu; it draws his boundary, then "upgrades" his highest wisdom into the RuJiang's way of co-opetition.

Five SunTzu Sayings and Their Common Business Misreadings

SunTzu's OriginalCommon MisreadingThe RuJiang's Transformation
"Warfare is the way of deception" 兵者，诡道也Business is fraud and trickeryBusiness may compete, but must not deceive
"Know the enemy and know yourself" 知彼知己Study only the opponentKnow self, other, righteousness, the terrain, and when to stop
"Subdue the enemy without fighting" 不战而屈人之兵Corner rivals by intrigueLower confrontation through credit, ecosystem, and cooperative space
"The supreme art is to attack stratagems; next, alliances" 上兵伐谋，其次伐交Strategy above all"Attacking alliances" means building them — the RuJiang builds alliances first
"Troops have no fixed formation; water no fixed shape" 兵无常势，水无常形No principles neededThe Doctrine of the Mean applied: principles fixed, methods fluid

The Fundamental Difference Between War and Business

DimensionWarBusiness
GoalDefeat and destroy the enemyProfit, cooperation, ecosystem-building
MeansKilling, deception, destructionValue creation, exchange, collaboration
TimeShort decisive battlesLong continuous operation
RelationsFriend versus foe, clearly drawnCompetition and cooperation interwoven
OutcomeOne side wins, the other losesWin-win, lose-lose, or prosper-together are all possible

Confucius: "The gentleman has no contentions... even in contention, they remain gentlemen." The highest realm of business competition is not eliminating the opponent — it is keeping the gentleman's bearing while competing.

The Real Cost of Zero-Sum Business War

When a company models business entirely on war, the consequences follow: involution — rivals undercut and attack each other until the whole industry's margin is compressed;

trust destruction — partners guard against each other and cooperation costs soar;

short-termism — defeating rivals today at the price of tomorrow's ecosystem;

reputation erosion — orders won, goodwill lost. The classic example: industry-wide price wars that end with every player losing money — no one truly wins.

SunTzu's "Subdue Without Fighting" Meets Confucian Co-opetition

SunTzu's highest wisdom is precisely "winning without fighting" — and this resonates deeply with the RuJiang's co-opetition.

SunTzu: "To subdue the enemy without fighting is the highest excellence."

Confucius: "If distant people do not submit, cultivate culture and virtue to attract them." 远人不服，则修文德以来之。

Mencius: "One who has the Way will have many supporters; one who has lost the Way will have few." 得道者多助，失道者寡助。

All three point to one conclusion: the best business victory is not eliminating the opponent — it is making the opponent unnecessary as an enemy.

The Paradigm Shift: From "Defeating Rivals" to "Growing Together with Partners"

Old Paradigm: Business-War ThinkingNew Paradigm: RuJiang Co-opetition
Goal: eliminate competitorsGoal: build a multi-party symbiotic ecosystem
The opponent is the enemyThe opponent may also be a partner
Cooperation is expedientCooperation is long-term strategy
Profit maximizationUnity of righteousness and profit; long-term value maximization
Trust is a weaknessTrust is the moat

Defining Co-opetition

Co-opetition (JingHe 竞合) is the fusion of competition and cooperation: market participants have conflicts of interest and common interests; not a fight to the death, but the pursuit of an ecosystem in which many parties can grow together.

The RuJiang co-opetition formula:

Co-opetition = Competitive Capability × Cooperative Credit × Long-Termism

Without competitive capability, you are eliminated. Without cooperative credit, you are isolated. Without long-termism, you trade away your future for short-term gain.

How Confucian Thought Derives Co-opetition

Co-opetition is not a patent of Western management theory — it is rooted deep in the Confucian classics.

(1) Ren 仁: respect all stakeholders.

"A benevolent person helps others establish what they wish to establish, and helps others achieve what they wish to achieve."

夫仁者，己欲立而立人，己欲达而达人。

— The Analects · Yong Ye

Ren is not kindness only to one's own; it is respect for every stakeholder. In business, Ren means "not treating the opponent as an enemy, but as a partner with whom one can grow."

(2) Shu 恕: empathy.

"Do not impose on others what you do not wish for yourself."

己所不欲，勿施于人。

— The Analects · Wei Ling Gong

If you do not want malicious price-squeezes, backstabbing, or broken contracts, do not do these to your rivals and partners. The first step of co-opetition is "treating others as people."

(3) The Doctrine of the Mean (ZhongYong 中庸): dynamic balance.

"The virtue of the mean is indeed the highest!"

中庸之为德也，其至矣乎！

— The Analects · Yong Ye

Co-opetition is not a fixed state but a dynamic balance — sometimes more competition, sometimes more cooperation. The key is never to slide to extremes.

From Zero-Sum to Positive-Sum: Mencius's "Human Harmony"

"Heavenly timing is not as good as earthly advantage; earthly advantage is not as good as human harmony."

天时不如地利，地利不如人和。

— Mencius · GongSun Chou II

The ultimate barrier in business competition is not technology or capital — it is human harmony (RenHe 人和). Where does it come from? From Confucius's reciprocity: do not impose on others what you do not wish for yourself. From the RuShang's Xin: a promise worth a thousand gold. From the RuJiang's Yi: the Way gathers supporters. So the RuJiang's goal is not "beating everyone" but "becoming the person most worth trusting, most worth partnering with."

The Four Levels of Co-opetition

LevelCharacterExample
L1 Adversarial rivalryFight to the death; zero-sumVicious price wars; mutual attacks
L2 Peaceful coexistenceSeparate markets; non-interferenceMarket segmentation; differentiated positioning
L3 Limited cooperationCooperating on shared interestsJoint procurement; co-building industry standards
L4 Ecosystem symbiosisCompetition and cooperation highly fusedPlatform ecosystems; supply-chain alliances

The RuJiang seeks the ascent from L1 toward L4.

Co-opetition Is Not Being a "Nice Guy"

One clarification: co-opetition does not mean perpetual concession. The RuJiang is not a pushover. He has full competitive capability — and keeps righteousness while competing. He is willing to cooperate — without abandoning his core interests. He pursues harmony — and dares to say "no" when necessary. Confucius said "harmony, not sameness" (HeErBuTong 和而不同): harmony is not uniformity, but balance achieved within difference.

SunTzu: "To win a hundred victories in a hundred battles is not the highest excellence." 百战百胜，非善之善者也。

Confucius: "Even in contention, they remain gentlemen." 君子无所争，必也射乎！

Mencius: "One who has the Way will have many supporters." 得道者多助，失道者寡助。

Confucius: "If distant people do not submit, cultivate culture and virtue to attract them." 远人不服，则修文德以来之。

Confucius: "The gentleman harmonizes without sameness; the petty person is the same without harmony." 君子和而不同，小人同而不和。

Mencius: "Heavenly timing is not as good as human harmony." 天时不如地利，地利不如人和。

SunTzu: "To subdue the enemy without fighting is the highest excellence." 不战而屈人之兵，善之善者也。

Inamori Kazuo: "The spirit of altruism." 利他之心。

  • List your company's confrontations with rivals; mark which can be converted into cooperative space.
  • Re-examine the goal "defeat the opponent": is it truly more important than "build the ecosystem"?
  • In management meetings, re-examine current competitive strategy using the war-versus-business difference table.
  • Read The Art of War · Attack by Stratagem; think through the real business path of "subduing without fighting."
  • Locate your key rival relationships on the four levels of co-opetition; find one "common interest point" and attempt limited cooperation.
  • Set "co-opetition boundaries": define what may be shared and what must be contested.
  • Retain core competitive capability inside every cooperation — avoid over-dependence on partners.
  • Co-opetition answers "how to live with rivals"; the Five Constants answer "with what capability one competes and cooperates."

Chapter 4.3.1 — Co-opetition, Turning Counterparties into Partners

"Do not impose on others what you do not wish for yourself."

己所不欲，勿施于人。

— The Analects · Wei Ling Gong

"Gentlemen harmonize while maintaining differences; petty persons are identical but cannot harmonize."

君子和而不同，小人同而不和。

— The Analects · Zi Lu

"When equilibrium and harmony are attained, heaven and earth take their proper positions, and all things flourish."

致中和，天地位焉，万物育焉。

— The Doctrine of the Mean (ZhongYong 中庸)

Negotiation is the RuJiang's most frequent battlefield. Adversarial negotiation chases "I win, you lose" — and usually destroys the long-term relationship. RuJiang negotiation pursues "harmony within difference; sustainable win-win."

Adversarial Negotiation versus RuJiang Win-Win Negotiation

DimensionAdversarial NegotiationRuJiang Win-Win Negotiation
GoalI win, you loseFind a scheme both sides can accept
TacticsConceal, pressure, threatenTransparency, empathy, creating options
RelationshipOne-time transactionLong-term partnership
OutcomeShort-term gain, long-term lossPossible short-term concession; long-term win-win

Empathy: The Power of "Do Not Impose on Others"

Before negotiating, stand on the other side's ground: their bottom line, their demands, their fears. This is not weakness — it is the precondition for finding the true zone of overlap. The RuJiang asks three questions before every negotiation:

If I were the other side, what would I fear most?

If I were the other side, what scheme would appeal to me?

How do I wish to be treated — and have I treated the other side the same way?

Harmony as the Highest Value: Finding the Third Path

"In the application of propriety, harmony is most valuable."

礼之用，和为贵。

— The Analects · Xue Er

When the two sides are sharply opposed, do not force the other into submission — creatively design a new middle path. The "third path" is not a split-the-difference compromise; it is a new point of value growth neither side had seen.

Distributing Benefits: Fairness and Sustainability

"When seeing profit, think of righteousness."

见利思义。

— The Analects · Xian Wen

Concede moderately in the short term to buy long-term, stable cooperation. The RuJiang knows: squeezing every drop from one deal may cost a decade's partner. Three principles of benefit distribution: fairness — let the other side feel respected; transparency — no back-room operations; sustainability — make the other side want to continue.

Case: A Cross-Border Negotiation in Practice

Background: A Chinese enterprise and a Southeast Asian supplier fell into contract dispute over rising raw-material prices. The supplier demanded a large increase or cut-off; acceptance would compress the buyer's margins severely.

The RuJiang solution:

Empathy: understood the supplier's cost pressure; no blaming.

Common interest: both wanted stable long-term relations — neither wanted cut-off or lost customers.

Third path: signed a "flexible price clause" — base price plus a floating mechanism — sharing risk on both sides.

Long-term alliance: the buyer helped the supplier raise efficiency; the supplier granted long-term priority of supply.

Result: The buyer paid somewhat more in the short term but gained stable supply, supplier loyalty, and long-term pricing advantage. The negotiation not only resolved the crisis — it upgraded the partnership.

Alliance: From Single Deals to Cross-Cycle Partnership

The RuJiang's ultimate strategy is not winning every skirmish but building a partner network that crosses cycles. A single deal earns money; a long-term partner earns the future.

DimensionSingle TransactionLong-Term Partner
GoalMaximize this dealCross-cycle co-creation of value
TimeShortLong
RelationshipAdversaryPartner
RiskHigh trust costLow trust cost
ReturnOne-offCompounding

The RuJiang gives priority to building long-term partners rather than re-gaming every transaction.

Screening Partners: Virtue and Talent Together 德才兼备

"Virtue is the commander of talent; talent is the support of virtue."

德者，才之帅；才者，德之资也。

— Sima Guang, Zizhi Tongjian (Comprehensive Mirror for Aid in Government)

The RuJiang screens partners not only for business strength but for values and integrity records. Talent without virtue is unfit for deep alliance. The four quadrants:

With VirtueWithout Virtue
With TalentBest partnerDangerous partner
Without TalentOne to developWalk away

Alliance Governance: Mechanisms That Survive Market Cycles

Long-term partnership is sustained not by sentiment but by mechanism. Three governance mechanisms of the RuJiang alliance:

Regular communication: fixed meetings; shared information platforms.

Early-warning of risk: identify risks ahead; respond together.

Flexible contracts: contracts that adjust with circumstances, not locks.

Reaching the World: Building a Multi-Win Ecosystem

"When poor, cultivate oneself alone; when successful, benefit the whole world."

穷则独善其身，达则兼济天下。

— Mencius · Jin Xin I

The RuJiang's alliances are not one-to-one only; they push upstream, downstream — even parts of the same industry — to build a benign ecosystem together. One enterprise succeeding is business; an ecosystem flourishing is civilization.

Case: A Cross-Border Alliance in Practice

Background: A Chinese enterprise entering the Southeast Asian market faced localization difficulties: unfamiliar channels, cultural distance, thin trust.

The RuJiang solution:

Choose a local partner of virtue and talent: not merely channel strength, but local reputation.

Build a "Confucian mode of cooperation": no coercive control — shared benefits, shared risks.

Let the partner win first: concede more profit early; help the partner grow; bank the trust.

Co-build the brand: build the brand together rather than merely exporting product.

Result: A distribution network stood up quickly; local brand credibility formed; long-term win-win was achieved.

Chapter Summary

All of the RuJiang's negotiation and alliance work rests on the inner moral base of the RuShang. Strip away the base and the tactics turn alien. Co-opetition is not a technique — it is a unified practice of inside and outside.

ZiGong: "One word for a whole life?" — "Is it not Reciprocity?" 终身行之者乎？其恕乎！

Sima Guang: "Virtue is the commander of talent." 德者，才之帅。

Confucius: "Do not impose on others what you do not wish for yourself." 己所不欲，勿施于人。

Confucius: "In the application of propriety, harmony is most valuable." 礼之用，和为贵。

The Doctrine of the Mean: "When equilibrium and harmony are attained, all things flourish." 致中和，天地位焉。

Confucius: "Within the four seas, all men are brothers." 四海之内，皆兄弟也。

Confucius: "Help others establish what you wish to establish." 己欲立而立人，己欲达而达人。

SunTzu: "At intersecting territory, form alliances." 衢地则合交。

Mencius: "When successful, benefit the whole world." 达则兼济天下。

  • Evaluate existing partners: distinguish "transactional relations" from "long-term alliances."
  • Set partner-screening criteria with integrity record as a hard indicator.
  • Design "let the partner win first" schemes — early concessions for long-term trust.
  • Push to build an industry ecosystem; do not watch only your own interest.
  • Negotiation and alliance are the discipline of good times; crisis is the touchstone of values.
  • Before negotiating, write down the three things the other side most likely fears.
  • Design the "third path": search for new value creation beyond both positions.
  • Build a negotiation review mechanism: after each negotiation, check whether the long-term relationship was damaged.
  • In cross-border contracts, add "flexibility clauses" and risk-sharing mechanisms.

Chapter 4.3.2 — RuJiang: Surviving Adversity and Crisis

"Only in the bitter cold does one realize the pine and the cypress are the last to wither."

岁寒，然后知松柏之后凋也。

— The Analects · Zi Han

"Do not do evil because it is small; do not leave good undone because it is small."

勿以恶小而为之，勿以善小而不为。

— LiuBei, Records of the Three Kingdoms · Annals of Shu

"I will bend my body to the task and exert myself to the utmost, until my dying day."

鞠躬尽瘁，死而后已。

— ZhugeLiang, Later Memorial on Dispatching the Troops

In good times, everyone claims to be a RuShang. In crisis, we finally see who is a RuJiang. Crisis is the touchstone of values.

Adversity and crisis lay bare the true character of values and human relationships. When danger strikes, the natural instinct is self-preservation — to protect one's own interests above all else. The RuJiang, however, holds a different view: survival is not merely about saving oneself.

For the RuJiang, enduring hardship means standing firm in xìn 信 (trustworthiness) and yì 义 (righteousness) even when pressure tempts compromise. Crisis demands that we safeguard the community: partners, team members, stakeholders and the wider network upon which everyone relies. Short-term personal gain gained by breaking faith will destroy the bonds needed for long-term recovery.

This aligns with the core RuDao philosophy of tǐ 体 (substance) and yòng 用 (function). Unchanging Confucian virtues form the substance; flexible, practical strategy is the function. The RuJiang adapts tactics to navigate turbulent conditions, yet never sacrifices integrity for an easy escape.

To uphold faith and righteousness amid crisis is how genuine, lasting trust is forged. This trust, built through shared hardship, becomes the most enduring resource to survive and rebuild after the storm passes.

Real historical examples

The Shanxi merchant bank Dade Tong (大德通) illustrates this RuJiang spirit. During the 1930 war, local currency collapsed and lost most of its value. The票号 (piaohao / draft bank) could have taken advantage of the collapse to reduce its obligations to depositors. Instead, its leaders chose to draw down decades of internal reserves and honour all deposits at the original agreed rate. This decision created a massive loss of 300,000 taels of silver and eventually led to the bank's closure, yet it preserved xìn 信 for its depositors and the broader community. The bank prioritised protecting ordinary people's savings over corporate survival, embodying the RuJiang idea that the community's trust matters more than short-term profit.

Another powerful example is Ruifuxiang (瑞蚨祥), the famous Shandong silk merchant house. In 1900, a huge fire in Beijing's Dashilan district burned its main store to rubble. Many competing shops closed or hid from their debts. The owner Meng Luochuan set up a makeshift tent shop amid the ruins and posted a public notice: all money owed by Ruifuxiang to customers would be fully repaid, while all debts owed to Ruifuxiang would be cancelled. Though this brought heavy immediate losses, his commitment to yì 义 won deep respect across the market.

These cases show the RuJiang logic: crisis creates temptation to protect only oneself. But the RuJiang accepts painful shortterm costs to defend the community and keep promises. Flexible operational choices are the yòng 用, but the moral anchor — xìn and yì — remains the unchanging tǐ 体.

Partners Are Easy in Prosperity; Adversary Reveals the True Ones

In boom times cooperation is easy, because the pie is growing. Only crisis tests the true quality of values and relationships. The RuJiang's view of crisis: crisis is not for self-preservation alone — it is for guarding the community. Keeping faith and righteousness amid crisis is what earns genuine long-term trust.

Three Principles of Crisis: Trust First, Righteousness Second, Profit Last

Xin first 信为先: never deceive or conceal; communicate transparently at once.

Yi second 义为重: rather bear short-term losses than injure partners.

Li last 利为后: once the crisis passes, profit returns on its own.

"A gentleman understands righteousness; a petty person understands only profit."

君子喻于义，小人喻于利。

— The Analects · Li Ren

Crisis most easily exposes "the petty person understands only profit": to save oneself, sacrifice partners. The RuJiang chooses "the gentleman understands righteousness": hold the bottom line; cross the river together.

Protecting Partners Inside the Crisis

The RuJiang's crisis action list: communicate at the first moment — no concealment; let partners know the true situation. Adjust terms proactively — within what you can bear, relieve partner pressure. Share risk — design mechanisms so partners do not absorb losses alone. Search for the way out together — solve it side by side, not each fleeing separately.

After the Crisis, Trust Is Sublimated

Partners who have crossed the storm together build a depth of trust no ordinary contract can produce. That trust is the strongest bedrock of future cooperation. Li Ka-shing said: "Reputation is my second life." To guard reputation inside crisis is to accumulate the strongest business capital for the future.

Historical Scenes: The RuJiang's Choice in Financial Crises, Pandemics, and Supply-Chain Breaks

Corporate BehaviorOrdinary EnterpriseRuJiang Enterprise
Paying suppliersDelay, compress, refuseShorten payment terms proactively; waive fees
Employee treatmentLayoffs, pay cuts, freezesCross the storm together; keep posts where possible
Customer serviceCut service, raise pricesKeep quality; concede margin to customers
InformationConceal, avoidTransparent, timely
Long-term resultShort-term self-preservation; partners lost in the endShort-term pressure; loyalty earned for the long run

Conclusion: those who self-preserve lose their partners; those who bear the pressure win loyalty. The table above maps the two endings, one by one.

Confucius: "Only in the bitter cold does one realize the pine and cypress are the last to wither." 岁寒，然后知松柏之后凋也。

LiuBei: "Do not do evil because it is small; do not leave good undone because it is small." 勿以恶小而为之，勿以善小而不为。

ZhugeLiang: "I will bend my body to the task, until my dying day." 鞠躬尽瘁，死而后已。

Confucius: "The gentleman understands righteousness; the petty person understands only profit." 君子喻于义，小人喻于利。

  • Draft a corporate crisis plan with "protecting partners" written into its principles.
  • Build a crisis-communication SOP ensuring first-moment transparency.
  • Run crisis simulations that test the team's moral choices under pressure.
  • Review after each crisis: were the three bottom lines — Xin, Yi, Li — held?
  • The crisis wisdom of commerce was demonstrated long ago. Return to the Three Kingdoms and take counsel from four ancient RuJiang.

Chapter 4.3.3 — Ancient RuJiang Wisdom: The Three Kingdoms

"Tranquility cultivates one's character; frugality nourishes one's virtue."

静以修身，俭以养德。

— ZhugeLiang, Admonition to My Son (JieZiShu 诫子书)

The ancient RuJiang were not bookish scholars who merely wielded the brush, but strategists who won people by virtue, won battles by wisdom, bore burdens by courage, and established standing by refinement. Their wisdom still guides business competition today.

ZhugeLiang 诸葛亮 (181–234): The Perfect Exemplar of the RuJiang

(1) Tranquility reaching far: strategic composure.

"Without frugality and detachment, one cannot clarify one's aspirations; without tranquility, one cannot reach far."

非淡泊无以明志，非宁静无以致远。

— ZhugeLiang, Admonition to My Son

The RuJiang is unmoved by short-term gain, and so reaches long-term goals. In the commercial sea, inner calm and composure are what let one see the whole board and plan for the long run.

(2) Devotion to the last breath: the RuJiang's sense of duty.

"I will bend my body to the task and exert myself to the utmost, until my dying day."

鞠躬尽瘁，死而后已。

— ZhugeLiang, Later Memorial

The highest professional spirit of the RuJiang — full commitment until the mission is done. Entrepreneurs should take ZhugeLiang as the model of loyalty to the cause and ownership of responsibility.

(3) The Empty Fort Strategy (KongChengJi 空城计): wisdom and daring.

The anecdote: ZhugeLiang held Xi City with a token garrison as Sima Yi's great army bore down. He threw the gates wide open, burned incense, and played his music instrument "qin琴" on the fortress tower, utterly composed. Sima Yi, suspecting an ambush, withdrew.

The RuJiang reading: the highest realm — "subduing the enemy without fighting." The Empty Fort is the perfect performance of SunTzu's dictum and the extreme expression of Confucian Zhi 智 and Yong 勇: dissolving the crisis, at the most desperate moment, with composure and wisdom.

(4) The core of ZhugeLiang's leadership. In The General's Garden (JiangYuan 将苑) he wrote:

"A general must not be arrogant. Arrogance leads to loss of propriety; loss of propriety leads to alienation of people; alienation leads to rebellion of the masses."

将不可骄，骄则失礼，失礼则人离，人离则众叛。

"Do not be arrogant with your talents, nor exert authority through favor."

不傲才以骄人，不以宠而作威。

Leaders must not swell with pride; they must keep humility and reverence.

ZhouYu 周瑜 (175–210): Refinement and Composure

"A true man in this world, meeting a lord who understands him, outwardly upholds the righteousness of ruler and minister, inwardly forms bonds of flesh and blood. His words are heeded, his plans followed, sharing both fortune and misfortune together."

大丈夫处世，遇知己之主，外托君臣之义，内结骨肉之恩，言听计从，祸福共之。

— ZhouYu

"Dispersing the enemy by playing music — qin" (TanQinTuiDi 弹琴退敌) — the RuJiang's bearing. On the eve of Red Cliffs, ZhouYu played his music instrument "qin琴" in his command tent, calm and unhurried. Enemy scouts, seeing this, judged that Wu must be fully prepared — and dared not move.

The RuJiang reading: true strategic confidence lies not in imposing display but in unshakable composure. ZhouYu at the qin and ZhugeLiang on the city wall are the same perfection — the RuJiang's wisdom, courage, and refinement (Zhi 智, Yong 勇, Ya 雅) as one body. Business lessons: share fortune and misfortune with partners — the RuJiang's loyalty; and genuine strategic confidence rests on inner composure.

LiuBei 刘备 (161–223): Winning People Through Virtue

"Only virtue and moral character can win the hearts of people."

惟贤惟德，能服于人。

— LiuBei, Records of the Three Kingdoms

"Cao Cao uses urgency; I use leniency. Cao Cao uses violence; I use benevolence. Cao Cao uses deception; I use loyalty. Wherever I am opposite to Cao Cao, there lies the path to success."

操以急，吾以宽；操以暴，吾以仁；操以谲，吾以忠: 每与操相反，事乃可成。

— LiuBei, Records of the Three Kingdoms

Business lessons: win people by virtue, not by force — the essence of leadership is winning hearts. Character shows in small things; vision shows in details — the RuShang's daily practice. And differentiation: build your advantage where the opponent is weak. LiuBei's "always opposite to Cao Cao" is the RuJiang's differentiated competition — where the rival uses urgency, violence, and deceit, answer with leniency, benevolence, and loyalty, and win hearts.

CaoCao 曹操 (155–220): Literary Cultivation and Political Wisdom

"Facing wine, let us sing — how long is human life? It is like morning dew — the days that have passed are many and bitter."

对酒当歌，人生几何！譬如朝露，去日苦多。

— CaoCao, Short Song Style (DuanGeXing 短歌行)

"The mountain never tires of being high; the sea never tires of being deep. Like the Duke of Zhou who spat out his food to receive visitors — may the hearts of all under heaven turn to me."

山不厌高，海不厌深。周公吐哺，天下归心。

— CaoCao, Short Song Style

Business lessons: cherish the present and seize the moment — the RuJiang's view of time. Recruit the talented widely and honor them — the RuJiang's breadth of mind. CaoCao is often painted as the "villain-hero" (JianXiong 奸雄), yet his heart of "the Duke of Zhou spatting out his food" — interrupting his own meal to greet talent — remains the model for leaders who would gather the best people.

Core conclusion: the wisdom of the ancient RuJiang — winning people by virtue, winning by wisdom, bearing by courage, standing by refinement — offers today's business leaders a complete spiritual map. The RuJiang is not a general; he is a strategist who commands commercial competition with Confucian wisdom.

ZhugeLiang: "Without tranquility, one cannot reach far." 非淡泊无以明志，非宁静无以致远。

LiuBei: "Only virtue and moral character can win the hearts of people." 惟贤惟德，能服于人。

  • Learn ZhugeLiang's "tranquility reaches far": reduce short-term anxiety; draft a ten-year strategy.
  • Learn LiuBei's "never skip the small good": build team trust from the details.
  • Learn CaoCao's "spatting out his food": build mechanisms that widely attract and honor talent.
  • Learn ZhouYu's "fortune shared": form communities of destiny with core partners.
  • Historical wisdom needs contemporary cases to come alive — four anonymized real cases to test the RuJiang's co-opetition in the field.

The RuJiang Casebook: Co-opetition

"The gentleman is slow in words but quick in action."

君子欲讷于言而敏于行。

— The Analects · Li Ren

"Troops have no fixed formation, just as water has no fixed shape."

兵无常势，水无常形。

— The Art of War · Xu Shi (Emptiness and Substance)

"To know but not to act is still not to know."

知而不行，只是未知。

— Wang Yangming

Theory only becomes learning when it lands. Through four anonymized real cross-border cases, this chapter shows how the RuJiang's co-opetition operates in real markets.

Case A — Co-opetition in Practice: When to Compete, When to Cooperate

Background: Two firms in the same track are competitors, yet face the same external supply-chain risk. Fight all-out and both bleed; cooperate fully and core interests collide.

The RuJiang approach: Define competitive boundaries — compete in the market, protect each one's core secrets. Find cooperative space — joint bargaining with upstream suppliers to cut common costs. Set co-opetition rules — sign a "co-opetition agreement" fixing the scope of cooperation and competition. Adjust dynamically — review the boundaries as the market shifts.

Result: The two firms gained cost advantage through cooperation while keeping market vitality through competition. This is "harmony without sameness" 和而不同 practiced in business.

Case B — The Choice in Crisis: Inner Values Decide the RuJiang Response

Background: The economy turns down; orders shrink. Compare two firms:

Company A squeezes supplier payments and cuts partner entitlements to protect its own statements. Short-term: a smaller profit decline. Long-term: supplier relations sour, industry reputation drops, future cooperation drains away.

Company B, holding its survival floor, negotiates shared pain with partners — extending payment terms proactively, waiving fees, sharing information. The statements look worse short-term, but partnerships hold firm; when the crisis passes, cooperation opportunities multiply.

Comparison: A's statement won short-term, but its ecosystem lost long-term. B's statement lost short-term, but its future won. This is "the gentleman understands righteousness, the petty person understands only profit" in its real-world edition.

Case C — Pseudo-Co-opetition Without RuShang Roots: Fine Strategy, Missing Virtue, Final Collapse

Background: A manager learns co-opetition rhetoric; outwardly cooperates while plotting to swallow the partner.

The process: Sign "strategic cooperation," promise joint market development. Secretly harvest the partner's customer data and poach its core team. When the moment ripens, tear up the cooperation and take the market alone.

Short-term result: gains captured; surface success. Long-term result: the story travels across industries; no credible alliance can be found afterwards. The firm is gradually isolated — and loses in a larger competition.

The lesson: co-opetition is inner-outer unity; it cannot serve as a surface rhetoric tool. Pseudo-co-opetition without a RuShang foundation eventually devours itself.

Case D — Cross-Cultural Business: The RuShang–RuJiang Paradigm Between East and West

Background: A Chinese enterprise partners with a European firm. Cultural distance: Western contract culture emphasizes legal text and explicit responsibility; the Confucian system emphasizes trust, relationships, and righteousness.

The collision: an ambiguous zone appears in execution. The Western partner insists on the letter; the Chinese side expects flexible handling.

The RuJiang solution: Respect the contract — execute by the letter; never challenge the legal floor. Build trust — beyond the contract, add regular communication and transparent disclosure. Cultural translation — render the Confucian Xin 信 as "Reliability" the Western side can grasp. Long-term fusion — infuse relational trust gradually, never abandoning the written contract.

Result: The two sides find balance between contract and trust; cooperation grows smoother.

The insight: the RuJiang does not reject contracts — he adds the dimension of trust on top of them. "Western rule frameworks + Eastern moral core" is the golden combination of cross-cultural cooperation.

Chapter Summary

All of the RuJiang's competition and cooperation strategies stand on the inner moral base of the RuShang. Without the base, tactics alienate. Co-opetition is not a technique — it is a unified cultivation of inside and outside.

Confucius: "Slow in words but quick in action." 君子欲讷于言而敏于行。

SunTzu: "Troops have no fixed formation; water no fixed shape." 兵无常势，水无常形。

Wang Yangming: "To know but not to act is still not to know." 知而不行，只是未知。

Confucius: "The gentleman harmonizes without sameness." 君子和而不同。

  • Analyze your biggest competitor now: find one "common interest point."
  • Recall one past crisis response: were the bottom lines of Xin, Yi, Li held?
  • Audit for pseudo-co-opetition: surface cooperation with inner scheming.
  • In cross-cultural cooperation, build the dual-track mechanism of "contract + trust."
Chapter 6.1

Confucian Trustworthiness and Web3, DAO, and the Digital Economy

RuDao in the Modern AI Digital Economy — with Dr. Zhu Jia-ming 朱嘉明

信

"A nation or enterprise cannot stand without trustworthiness."

民无信不立

The Analects · Yan Yuan

"A person without trustworthiness — I do not know what they are good for."

人而无信 不知其可也

The Analects · Wei Zheng

"Know the enemy and know yourself, and you will not be imperiled in a hundred battles."

知彼知己 百战不殆

The Art of War · Mou Gong

"The spirit of altruism."

利他之心

Inamori Kazuo

Web3 brings decentralization, blockchain, DAOs, and the token economy. But technological decentralization does not equal decentralized trust. Quite the opposite: the Web3 era needs Xin 信 as its underlying ethics more than ever.

The Capability Boundary of the Blockchain: It Stores Records; It Cannot Guarantee Truthful Input

Cryptography guarantees that what is on-chain cannot be tampered with — it cannot guarantee that the data entered is true. Technical trust requires the human moral motive (the RuShang's Xin) as its precondition. False information written on-chain simply becomes a permanent lie.

Therefore: the credit system of the blockchain era = technical trust + moral motive. (For the supply-chain and cross-border practice of Xin, see Sections 5.3–5.5.)

Chapter 6.1.1 — RuDao in the Modern AI Digital Economy: Dr. Zhu JiaMing

儒道在现代AI数字经济中——朱嘉明博士

Dr. Zhu JiaMing, Reformer, Scholar, My Teacher & Dear Friend

Dr. Zhu JiaMing (朱嘉明博士教授) is my dear friend, my trusted colleague, and above all — my teacher.

He serves with me as Honorary Chairman of the Hong Kong Blockchain Association (HKBA.cc 香港区块链协会荣誉会长). But the title tells only a small part of the story. To me, Dr. Zhu is one of the most respected reformer-scholars of our time — a man who contributed to China's modernization from Central Planning to Market Economy. Dr. Zhu is not only as a scholar, but as an architect of ideas and a trusted academic advisor to government who sow the seeds and help drafted the architecture plan for the great reform and modernization of China. His journey spans from the reform generation of the 1980s to the AI-Web3 frontier of the twenty-first century, and at every stage, he has remained a teacher -- patient, generous, and profoundly insightful.

For the last ten years, Dr. Zhu has served as Honorary Chairman of HKBA, and in that role he has given me and our association an extraordinary gift: sustained research guidance, strategic advisory, and education on AI, blockchain, tokenization of real world assets RWA, Web3, Web4, and the digital economy across Hong Kong, Macau, Hengqin Zhuhai, and the Greater Bay Area of China (粤港澳大湾区).

Born in Beijing in 1950, Dr. Zhu received his PhD in economics from the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences (中国社会科学院) in 1987. He was a visiting scholar at Harvard, MIT, Manchester, and Tufts, served as an economist at the United Nations Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO), and taught at the University of Vienna and National Taiwan University. He is one of the "Four Gentlemen of Reform" (改革四君子) of the 1980s, alongside Weng Yongxi, Wang Qishan, and Huang Jiangnan, and a key organizer of the Moganshan Conference (莫干山会议) — the very gathering that helped shape China's reform-era economic thinking.

But what moves me most is not the list of titles. It is the way Dr. Zhu teaches. He does not lecture from a distance. He walks with you. He asks questions that open new doors. He shares his research before it is published, his doubts before they are resolved, and his vision before it is fully formed. In the Confucian tradition, this is the mark of a true teacher — 师者 所以传道授业解惑也 (a teacher is one who transmits the Way, imparts knowledge, and resolves doubts).

This chapter is my attempt to honor that teaching. It argues that Dr. Zhu's research on AI, blockchain, the digital economy, and Web3 provides the strongest theoretical bridge between the ancient Confucian wisdom of RuDao (儒道) and the modern intelligent economy (智能经济).

AI as a New Species 新物种: 和而不同

Dr. Zhu's most important judgment about artificial intelligence is this: AI is not an ordinary tool. It is a new species (新物种) reshaping the very form of industry.

In his 2025 book The Third Existence: From General Intelligence to Super Intelligence (《第三种存在: 从通用智能到超级智能》), he proposes that human civilization has been built on two forms of existence: physical-material existence and spiritual-consciousness existence. AI creates a third existence (第三种存在) — a complex ecosystem of "AI + human + environment."

This resonates deeply with the RuDao principle of 和而不同 (harmony in diversity). Confucianism does not seek conquest. It seeks coexistence: "万物并育而不相害" — all things grow together without harming one another. When AI becomes a new economic subject, the entrepreneur's core question is no longer "How do I defeat my competitor?" but "How do I co-opetition (竞合) with AI and the new technological ecosystem?"

This is the digital extension of 天人合一 (harmony between Heaven and humanity). The relationship between Heaven and humanity is no longer only about nature and people. It now includes humans and intelligent agents, humans and algorithms, humans and digital ecosystems.

Web3 + AI: 诚信为本 as Programmable Trust

In September 2024, at the Bund Conference (外滩大会), Dr. Zhu delivered a keynote titled Web3 + AI: Reconstructing Future Industries (《Web3 + AI: 重构未来产业》). His conclusion was clear:

The core value of Web3 must reconnect with its original essence. Its network must combine with AI to achieve human-machine coordination (人机协调).

He pointed out that Web3 has long been "盛名之下，其实难副" — great reputation, little substance — with limited impact on the real economy. Without AI's full penetration, Web3 has no vitality. He proposed five ways Web3 can combine with AI:

Introduce AI large models to improve Web3's computing power and algorithms for data processing.

Achieve "co-governance" (共治) between AI and natural intelligence.

Build a "community" (共同体) between humans and embodied intelligence (具身智能).

Promote the integration of AI with Web3 tokens.

Support industrial reconstruction based on the Web3 framework.

He emphasized: Token will connect AI and Web3, realizing the long-awaited tokenization and giving Web3 a real value foundation.

From the RuDao perspective, blockchain is 用技术手段实现无需信任的信任 — using technology to achieve trust without requiring trust. Confucianism says 诚信为本 (integrity as the foundation). Smart contracts, on-chain records, and immutable distributed ledgers elevate integrity from personal morality to systemic institutional design.

In the Web3 + AI context, RuDao's 义利统一 (unity of righteousness and profit) gains a new path: transparent rules and self-executing contracts make 以义取利 (seeking profit through righteousness) not merely a moral slogan, but a programmable engineering practice.

The End of Traditional Business Cycles: 因时制宜

In a 2026 interview with National Business Daily (每日经济新闻), Dr. Zhu made a striking claim:

Traditional business cycles are gone (传统经济周期已荡然无存).

He explained that in the 18 years since the 2008 financial crisis, classic cycle theories — Kitchin, Juglar, Kuznets — have failed to explain current economic reality. The traditional "boom—bust—recession—recovery" pattern has largely disappeared. AI has brought the technology cycle and innovation cycle into the traditional economic system, making the innovation cycle the dominant cycle.

For entrepreneurs, this is a profound strategic shift. If traditional cycles no longer work, the new capability is not "predicting cycles." It is "coexisting with uncertainty" (与不确定性共生) — precisely the modern expression of 因时制宜 (adapting to the times) and 中庸 (Doctrine of the Mean).

RuDao's 长期主义 (long-termism) must be redefined in the intelligent economy. It is no longer "the accumulation of multiple business cycles." It is "innovation resilience in coexistence with uncertainty".

Ethics and Profit: 以义取利

In a dialogue with He Huaihong (何怀宏), Dr. Zhu stated clearly:

Public good cannot be achieved through greed, shamelessness, and selfishness. The cost of such "prosperity" is more than most people can bear. Society must have moral standards.

He also noted that Adam Smith's "invisible hand" has been vulgarized and absolutized by later generations. The real Adam Smith had a broader vision.

This aligns closely with RuDao's 以义取利 (seeking profit through righteousness). Confucianism never denies profit. It insists that profit must be pursued within a framework of righteousness. Dr. Zhu's rejection of "prosperity through vice" is the academic expression of 义利统一 (unity of righteousness and profit) in economic discourse.

Blockchain and Enduring Partnerships: 长久伙伴关系

In 2020, at a United Nations blockchain conference, Dr. Zhu delivered a keynote titled Facing the Global Trust Crisis and Trust Deficit: Blockchain Will Be the Infrastructure for Rebuilding Human Trust (《积极面对全球性信任危机和信任赤字的挑战——区块链将是重建人类信任的基础结构》).

He argued that blockchain is not merely a technology. It is a new trust infrastructure (信任基础结构). It can change the trust mechanism of the industrial economy, the organizational system from factory to company, and the nature of employment.

This directly echoes RuDao's 长久伙伴关系 (enduring partnerships). Confucianism says 诚信为本 (integrity as the foundation). Blockchain is 用技术手段实现无需信任的信任 — using technology to achieve trust without relying on personal trust. When smart contracts replace verbal promises and on-chain records replace paper contracts, RuDao's 义利统一 is transforming from an ethical proposition into an engineering problem.

This is one of Dr. Zhu's most important contributions to the RuDao framework.

RuDao Synthesis: Five Principles for the AI Digital Economy

From Dr. Zhu's research, we can distill five RuDao principles for the modern AI digital economy:

PrincipleChinese KeywordApplication in AI / Web3
Harmony in diversity和而不同Co-opetition with AI, algorithms, and digital ecosystems
Integrity as foundation诚信为本Blockchain and smart contracts as programmable trust
Adapt to the times因时制宜Innovation cycle replaces traditional business cycles
Unity of righteousness and profit义利统一Transparent rules and tokenization make ethics executable
Harmony between Heaven and humanity天人合一Human-AI-environment as one complex ecosystem

These five principles form a RuDao checklist for leaders in the intelligent economy:

Are we competing with AI, or co-opetition (竞合) with it?

Is our trust model personal, institutional, or programmable?

Are we predicting cycles, or building resilience for uncertainty?

Is our profit model righteous by design?

Are we treating AI as a tool, or as a new species (新物种) in a shared ecosystem?

Conclusion: A Teacher's Wisdom for the Intelligent Age

Dr. Zhu JiaMing's academic thought gives RuDao a crucial dimension: technology is not the opposite of RuDao. It is the extension of RuDao into a new field.

When AI becomes a new economic species, when the digital economy merges into the intelligent economy (智能经济), and when Web3 reconstructs trust mechanisms, Confucian wisdom — 竞合 (co-opetition), 义利统一, 和而不同, 诚信为本 — provides the value anchor the technological age most needs.

Technology becomes more powerful; ethics becomes more necessary. Algorithms become more efficient; human wisdom becomes more necessary to define "what is right."

RuDao is not nostalgia. It is a future-oriented Eastern business philosophy. In the age of AI and Web3, the RuDao question is no longer only "How do I become a good merchant?" It is:

How do I become a righteous participant in an economic ecosystem shared with intelligent agents?

This is the path I advocate in RuDao: Beyond The Art of War — the Confucian Co-opetition Way to Balanced Profit and Enduring Partnerships. Dr. Zhu JiaMing's thought provides the most solid theoretical argument and era coordinates for this path.

But beyond the theory, beyond the frameworks and the citations, there is something simpler and more enduring: the gratitude of a student for his teacher. Dr. Zhu has taught me not only how to think about AI and Web3, but how to think about them with a Confucian conscience. He has shown me that the greatest technological revolutions do not replace wisdom — they demand it.

For that, I am forever grateful.

谢谢您，朱老师。

RuDao in the Modern AI Digital Economy: Dr. Zhu JiaMing

儒道在现代AI数字经济中——朱嘉明博士

Author: Tony Tong 唐儀 RuDao — Beyond The Art of War: The Confucian Co-opetition Way to Balanced Profit and Enduring Partnerships 儒道——超越孫子兵法——平衡利潤與長久夥伴關係的儒家競合之道

A Scholar at the Crossroads of Reform and Digital Revolution

Dr. Zhu JiaMing (朱嘉明博士教授) is not only an economist. He is a bridge between two eras: the reform generation of 1980s China and the AI-Web3 frontier of the twenty-first century.

Born in Beijing in 1950, Dr. Zhu received his PhD in economics from the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences (中国社会科学院) in 1987. He was a visiting scholar at Harvard, MIT, Manchester, and Tufts, served as an economist at the United Nations Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO), and taught at the University of Vienna and National Taiwan University. He is one of the "Four Gentlemen of Reform" (改革四君子) of the 1980s, alongside Weng Yongxi, Wang Qishan, and Huang Jiangnan, and a key organizer of the Moganshan Conference (莫干山会议).

Today, Dr. Zhu serves as Honorary Chairman of the Hong Kong Blockchain Association (HKBA.cc 香港区块链协会荣誉会长). HKBA is one of Hong Kong's most influential blockchain industry organizations, co-founded by Tony Tong (唐儀), who serves as co-founder and executive president. In 2023, HKBA established the Qianhai WEB3HUB (前海WEB3HUB) in Shenzhen to provide Web3 consulting services for enterprises.

This chapter argues that Dr. Zhu's research on AI, blockchain, the digital economy, and Web3 provides the strongest theoretical bridge between the ancient Confucian wisdom of RuDao (儒道) and the modern intelligent economy (智能经济).

AI as a New Species 新物种: 和而不同

Dr. Zhu's most important judgment about artificial intelligence is this: AI is not an ordinary tool. It is a new species (新物种) reshaping the very form of industry.

In his 2025 book The Third Existence: From General Intelligence to Super Intelligence (《第三种存在: 从通用智能到超级智能》), he proposes that human civilization has been built on two forms of existence: physical-material existence and spiritual-consciousness existence. AI creates a third existence (第三种存在) — a complex ecosystem of "AI + human + environment."

This resonates deeply with the RuDao principle of 和而不同 (harmony in diversity). Confucianism does not seek conquest. It seeks coexistence: "万物并育而不相害" — all things grow together without harming one another. When AI becomes a new economic subject, the entrepreneur's core question is no longer "How do I defeat my competitor?" but "How do I co-opetition (竞合) with AI and the new technological ecosystem?"

This is the digital extension of 天人合一 (harmony between Heaven and humanity). The relationship between Heaven and humanity is no longer only about nature and people. It now includes humans and intelligent agents, humans and algorithms, humans and digital ecosystems.

Web3 + AI: 诚信为本 as Programmable Trust

In September 2024, at the Bund Conference (外滩大会), Dr. Zhu delivered a keynote titled Web3 + AI: Reconstructing Future Industries (《Web3 + AI: 重构未来产业》). His conclusion was clear:

The core value of Web3 must reconnect with its original essence. Its network must combine with AI to achieve human-machine coordination (人机协调).

He pointed out that Web3 has long been "盛名之下，其实难副" — great reputation, little substance — with limited impact on the real economy. Without AI's full penetration, Web3 has no vitality. He proposed five ways Web3 can combine with AI:

Introduce AI large models to improve Web3's computing power and algorithms for data processing.

Achieve "co-governance" (共治) between AI and natural intelligence.

Build a "community" (共同体) between humans and embodied intelligence (具身智能).

Promote the integration of AI with Web3 tokens.

Support industrial reconstruction based on the Web3 framework.

He emphasized: Token will connect AI and Web3, realizing the long-awaited tokenization and giving Web3 a real value foundation.

From the RuDao perspective, blockchain is 用技术手段实现无需信任的信任 — using technology to achieve trust without requiring trust. Confucianism says 诚信为本 (integrity as the foundation). Smart contracts, on-chain records, and immutable distributed ledgers elevate integrity from personal morality to systemic institutional design.

In the Web3 + AI context, RuDao's 义利统一 (unity of righteousness and profit) gains a new path: transparent rules and self-executing contracts make 以义取利 (seeking profit through righteousness) not merely a moral slogan, but a programmable engineering practice.

The End of Traditional Business Cycles: 因时制宜

In a 2026 interview with National Business Daily (每日经济新闻), Dr. Zhu made a striking claim:

Traditional business cycles are gone (传统经济周期已荡然无存).

He explained that in the 18 years since the 2008 financial crisis, classic cycle theories — Kitchin, Juglar, Kuznets — have failed to explain current economic reality. The traditional "boom—bust—recession—recovery" pattern has largely disappeared. AI has brought the technology cycle and innovation cycle into the traditional economic system, making the innovation cycle the dominant cycle.

For entrepreneurs, this is a profound strategic shift. If traditional cycles no longer work, the new capability is not "predicting cycles." It is "coexisting with uncertainty" (与不确定性共生) — precisely the modern expression of 因时制宜 (adapting to the times) and 中庸 (Doctrine of the Mean).

RuDao's 长期主义 (long-termism) must be redefined in the intelligent economy. It is no longer "the accumulation of multiple business cycles." It is "innovation resilience in coexistence with uncertainty".

Ethics and Profit: 以义取利

In a dialogue with He Huaihong (何怀宏), Dr. Zhu stated clearly:

Public good cannot be achieved through greed, shamelessness, and selfishness. The cost of such "prosperity" is more than most people can bear. Society must have moral standards.

He also noted that Adam Smith's "invisible hand" has been vulgarized and absolutized by later generations. The real Adam Smith had a broader vision.

This aligns closely with RuDao's 以义取利 (seeking profit through righteousness). Confucianism never denies profit. It insists that profit must be pursued within a framework of righteousness. Dr. Zhu's rejection of "prosperity through vice" is the academic expression of 义利统一 (unity of righteousness and profit) in economic discourse.

Blockchain and Enduring Partnerships: 长久伙伴关系

In 2020, at a United Nations blockchain conference, Dr. Zhu delivered a keynote titled Facing the Global Trust Crisis and Trust Deficit: Blockchain Will Be the Infrastructure for Rebuilding Human Trust (《积极面对全球性信任危机和信任赤字的挑战——区块链将是重建人类信任的基础结构》).

He argued that blockchain is not merely a technology. It is a new trust infrastructure (信任基础结构). It can change the trust mechanism of the industrial economy, the organizational system from factory to company, and the nature of employment.

This directly echoes RuDao's 长久伙伴关系 (enduring partnerships). Confucianism says 诚信为本 (integrity as the foundation). Blockchain is 用技术手段实现无需信任的信任 — using technology to achieve trust without relying on personal trust. When smart contracts replace verbal promises and on-chain records replace paper contracts, RuDao's 义利统一 is transforming from an ethical proposition into an engineering problem.

This is one of Dr. Zhu's most important contributions to the RuDao framework.

RuDao Synthesis: Five Principles for the AI Digital Economy

From Dr. Zhu's research, we can distill five RuDao principles for the modern AI digital economy:

PrincipleChinese KeywordApplication in AI / Web3
Harmony in diversity和而不同Co-opetition with AI, algorithms, and digital ecosystems
Integrity as foundation诚信为本Blockchain and smart contracts as programmable trust
Adapt to the times因时制宜Innovation cycle replaces traditional business cycles
Unity of righteousness and profit义利统一Transparent rules and tokenization make ethics executable
Harmony between Heaven and humanity天人合一Human-AI-environment as one complex ecosystem

These five principles form a RuDao checklist for leaders in the intelligent economy:

Are we competing with AI, or co-opetition (竞合) with it?

Is our trust model personal, institutional, or programmable?

Are we predicting cycles, or building resilience for uncertainty?

Is our profit model righteous by design?

Are we treating AI as a tool, or as a new species (新物种) in a shared ecosystem?

Conclusion: RuDao as a Future-Oriented Eastern Business Philosophy

Dr. Zhu JiaMing's academic thought gives RuDao a crucial dimension: technology is not the opposite of RuDao. It is the extension of RuDao into a new field.

When AI becomes a new economic species, when the digital economy merges into the intelligent economy (智能经济), and when Web3 reconstructs trust mechanisms, Confucian wisdom — 竞合 (co-opetition), 义利统一, 和而不同, 诚信为本 — provides the value anchor the technological age most needs.

Technology becomes more powerful; ethics becomes more necessary. Algorithms become more efficient; human wisdom becomes more necessary to define "what is right."

RuDao is not nostalgia. It is a future-oriented Eastern business philosophy. In the age of AI and Web3, the RuDao question is no longer only "How do I become a good merchant?" It is:

How do I become a righteous participant in an economic ecosystem shared with intelligent agents?

This is the path Tony Tong (唐儀) advocates in RuDao: Beyond The Art of War — the Confucian Co-opetition Way to Balanced Profit and Enduring Partnerships. Dr. Zhu JiaMing's thought provides the most solid theoretical argument and era coordinates for this path.

儒道 RuDao — 增补章节

朱嘉明教授: 香港区块链协会荣誉会长、AI·Web3·数字经济学术思想与儒道的时代共振

一、朱嘉明教授与香港区块链协会（HKBA.cc）

朱嘉明教授现任香港区块链协会（HKBA.cc）荣誉会长。HKBA是香港最具影响力的区块链行业组织之一，由唐仪（Tony Tong）担任联合创始人兼执行会长。协会荣誉主席还包括香港数码港董事局前主席林家礼博士、香港立法会议员邱达根、前港区全国人大代表张俊勇、美图创始人蔡文胜等。2023年，HKBA在深圳前海港合作区成立前海WEB3HUB，对外开展Web3领域的企业咨询服务。

朱嘉明教授作为荣誉会长，为协会提供了坚实的学术理论支撑。自2010年以来，他一直关注并深入研究加密数字货币和区块链的发展，是国内最早系统研究该领域的主流经济学家之一。他与唐仪会长在HKBA的共事关系，构成了《儒道 RuDao》在"学术—产业—政策"之间的重要连接纽带。

二、朱嘉明教授核心学术观点与儒道的对话

（一）AI不是工具，是"新物种"——儒道"和而不同"的当代扩展

朱嘉明关于人工智能最核心的判断是: AI不是普通技术工具，而是正在重塑产业形态的"新物种" 。2025年，他在《第三种存在: 从通用智能到超级智能》中系统提出，数千年人类文明基于两种存在——物理性和物质的存在、精神和意识的存在；而人工智能所创造的"第三种存在"，是"人工智能+人+环境"的复杂生态系统。

这一判断与儒道"和而不同"的核心精神形成深层共振。儒家讲共生而非征服，讲"万物并育而不相害"。当AI成为经济活动的新主体时，企业家面临的核心问题不再是"如何战胜竞争对手"，而是如何与AI、与新技术生态实现竞合共生。这正是儒道"天人合一"思想在数字时代的延伸——天人关系不再仅限于人与自然，还包括人与智能体、人与算法、人与数字生态的关系。

（二）Web3 + AI: 重构未来产业——儒道"诚信为本"的技术实现

2024年9月，朱嘉明在外滩大会发表《Web3 + AI: 重构未来产业》主题演讲，明确提出: Web3的核心价值需联系原教旨，其网络必须和人工智能结合，实现人机协调。他指出，Web3长期以来处于"盛名之下，其实难副"的状态，对实体经济的影响"风声大、雨点小"；没有AI全面渗透影响的Web3是没有生命力的。

朱嘉明提出Web3可以从五个方面与AI结合: 引入AI大模型改善Web3处理数据的算力和算法；实现AI和自然智能的"共治"；构建人类与具身智能的"共同体"；推动AI与Web3 Token的融合；支持基于Web3框架的产业重构。他特别强调，Token将把人工智能和Web3连在一起，实现人们期望已久的tokenization，真正赋予Web3以真实的价值基础。

从儒道视角看，区块链的本质是用技术手段实现无需信任的信任。儒家讲"诚信为本"，而智能合约、链上记录、不可篡改的分布式账本，正在将"诚信"从个人道德层面提升为系统性的制度设计。儒道所追求的"义利统一"，在Web3+AI的语境下获得了新的实现路径: 透明的规则、自动执行的合约，让"以义取利"不再是道德号召，而成为可编程的工程实践。

（三）传统经济周期已荡然无存——儒道"因时制宜"的战略重构

朱嘉明在2026年接受《每日经济新闻》专访时抛出震撼学界的判断: 传统经济周期已荡然无存。他指出，自2008年金融危机之后的18年间，基钦周期、朱格拉周期、库兹涅茨周期等经典周期理论已无法解释当下经济运行；AI将科技周期、创新周期引入传统经济体系，使技术创新周期成为主导周期。

这一判断对企业战略的启示是深远的。如果传统周期已经失效，企业家需要的新能力不是"预测周期"，而是"与不确定性共生"——这恰恰是儒家"因时制宜""中庸平衡"智慧的现代演绎。儒道的"长期主义"，在智能经济时代需要重新定义为"与不确定性共存的创新韧性"，而非"多个商业周期的叠加"。

（四）商业伦理与道德标准——儒道"以义取利"的学术印证

朱嘉明在与何怀宏的对谈中明确表示，公益不能基于贪婪、无耻、自私来实现客观上的繁荣，因为这样的成本不是大多数人能承受的，社会道德应当有标准。他同时指出，亚当·斯密的"看不见的手"思想被后人庸俗化和绝对地放大了，真实的亚当·斯密视野更为辽阔。

这一观点与儒道"以义取利"的核心理念高度一致。儒家从不否定利益，但强调利润必须在道义框架内获取。朱嘉明对"恶德繁荣论"的否定，正是儒道"义利统一"在经济学话语体系中的学术表达。

（五）区块链重建信任基础结构——儒道"长久伙伴关系"的技术底座

朱嘉明在2020年联合国区块链会议上发表主题演讲《积极面对全球性信任危机和信任赤字的挑战——区块链将是重建人类信任的基础结构》。他指出，区块链不仅是一种技术，更是一种新的信任基础结构，可以改变工业经济的信任机制、从工厂到公司的组织体制以及就业方式。

这与《儒道》的"长久伙伴关系"命题形成直接呼应。儒家"诚信为本"，而区块链正是用技术手段实现无需信任的信任。当智能合约替代口头承诺，当链上记录替代纸质契约，儒道所追求的"义利统一"正在从伦理命题转变为工程问题——这是朱嘉明学术思想对儒道框架最重要的贡献之一。

三、朱嘉明教授简介

朱嘉明，1950年生于北京，1987年获中国社会科学院经济学博士学位。哈佛大学、麻省理工学院、曼彻斯特大学、塔夫茨大学访问学者，曾任联合国工业发展组织经济学家，先后执教于维也纳大学和台湾大学。他是上世纪80年代"改革四君子"之一（与翁永曦、王岐山、黄江南并称），也是莫干山会议的主要组织者和发起者之一。

现任职务:

— 香港区块链协会（HKBA.cc）荣誉会长

— 横琴粤澳深度合作区数链数字金融研究院学术与技术委员会主席

— 数字资产研究院学术与技术委员会主席、院长

— 中国投资协会数字资产研究中心专家组组长

— "两岸区块链联盟"荣誉主席

— 澳门产业区块链协会会长

— "阳明山未来学社"创始人
